DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Department's 7, January 16, 1 p.m.
One. There have been no important financial develop-
ments during the past week. Interbank payments on the
basis of instructions received from abroad are being
894.51/477
resumed but no (repeat no) import exchange has been
settled since the enforcement of the exchange regulations
on January 8. The banks are requiring importers to pro-
duce figures of their imports of commodities in 1935 and
1936 before consideration is given their import bills.
This appears to be an effort to gain time but it may react
unfavorably against the yen when the importers having ful-
JAN 22 1937
filled the requirements present import bills in large
FILED
quantities for settlement. In the meantime the Yokohama
Specie Bank has succeeded in holding the yen to the an-
nounced rate of one shilling two pence and events indicate
that it will be able to hold the rate at this level for
some time.
F/FG
Two. The fundamental factors weakening the financial
structure, however, have not changed. Prices are con-
tinuing to rise rapidly in anticipation of credit inflation
and

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
 #9, Jan. 18, 7 p.m. from Tokyo
and of the inability of economic resources to meet the
demmds of the budget. The inordinately rapid rise in
prices is enhancing the uneasiness felt in economic circles.
It is increasingly evident that the tax inc rease plan will
be opposed in the Diet by the political parties (backed
by the financial and industrial interests) but it is not
believed that the opposition will succeed in upsetting the
tudgetary and other financial arrangements made by the
oovornment.
Three. It is rumored that Fukai conservative Governor
of the Bank of Japan will resign because of differences
of opinion with Baba Minister of Finance. It is
assumed in financial circles that the differences of
opinion are concerned with the forcing of deficit bonds on
the Bank of Japan.
(NO SIGNATURE)

894.51
17, January 23, 2 p.m.
FOR THE SECRETARY OF THE TREASURY
The political crisis in Japan has had no effect as
894.51/479
yet on the exchange value of the yen, which remains steady
ad has even shown increased firmness against sterling
since the crisis developed. The control of the yen
exchange is apparently operating effectively to prevent
substantial fluctuations even during disturbed periods.
Prices of government bonds have also remained firm but in-
dustrial stock prices slumped heavily yesterday, apparently
due to a fear that collapse of the Hirota Cabinet would
FILED
lead to the formation of a new Cabinet more under military
domination and therefore more inclined to governmental
control of industry and finance than is the present
Cabinet. The stock market however was somewhat firmer
this morning
F/FG
GREW

January 19, 1957:
CONVERSATION:
Division 
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Mr. Katsuo Okazaki,
REOEIVED
Chy sent to Jokyo 
Mr. Dooman.
Department of State
JAN 2 9 1937
Mr. Okazaki showed Mr. Dooman a bundle of olippings
from this morning's issues of the NEW YORK TIMES, HERALD
894.51/480
TRIBUNE, and other leading papers, of despatches under a
Washington dateline with regard to an interview given
yesterday by a "high official of the Treasury Department",
in whioh extended reference was made to the recent Japa-
nese Exchange Control Law. Mr. Okazaki said that the
894.5151
press reports had been cabled to Japan and had created
considerable uneasiness. He said that he did not know
whether the "high offioial of the Treasury Department"
referred to the Seoretary of the Treasury, but that he
FELLD
did know that the Secretary of the Treasury did give an
FEB 5
interview yesterday to press correspondents. Mr. Okazaki
said that he was not calling on instructions from his
Government, but that the representatives in New York of
the leading Japanese banks had requested the Embassy to
take such action as it could to remove the misapprehen-
sions created by the accounts of the Treasury interview.
Mr. Okasaki went on to say that the press despatohes
F/FG
give rise to the following impressions in the mind of
the average reader:
、1
1. That

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
1. That the Japanese Exchange Control
operations were being carried out with great
seorecy;
2. That Exchange Control operations created
obstacles to the conduct of business in Japan
by Americans; and
3. That Japan may devalue its currency.
With regard to the first point, Mr. Okazaki said that
the Exchange Control operations were be conducted in
accordance "with a law" which, he said, was promulgated
early this month, and that the text of the law is readily
available. He said that Mr. Ukawa, of the Japanese
Financial Attaché's office in New York, would arrive in
Washington today with the text in translation of the law.
With regard to the second point, Mr. Okazaki said
that drafts on foreign financial centers would be issued
in Japan, provided that the amounts covered bona fide
business transactions. No doubt, he added, certain
formalities would have to be observed, but it is unlikely
that the observance of these formalities would constitute
any obstacle to the transaction of business in Japan.
With regard to the third point, Mr. Okazaki said
that the suggestion that Japan would devalue its currency
is entirely without foundation. The purpose of the
Exchange Control is to stabilize Japanese currency in
terms of foreign exchange.
Mr. Okazaki said that Mr. Ukawa hoped to have an
opportunity on Thursday, January 21, to inform responsible
officers

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
officers of this Government with regard to the operation
of the recent Japanese Exchange Control measure, and that
it would be appreciated if, after the information was
made available, the Treasury would issue to the public
some statement calculated to remove the misunderstanding
on the part of the general public arising out of the
interview given to the press yesterday.
Mr. Dooman said that he would look into the matter
as soon as possible, and that he would communicate with
Mr. Okazaki on Thursday morning.
m.m.A.

January 21, 1937.
57
Mr. Ukawa,
Assistant to the Japanese
Financial Attaché,
Mr. Katsuo Okazaki,
Second Secretary,
DEP.
Japanese Embassy.
Division o
FAR EASTERN AFLATHS
JAN 2 9 1937 Mr. Vayne Taylor,
4FEB1-1937
Assistant Secretary of the Treasury
Department of State
Mr. Dooman.
894.51/481
In accordance with an arrangement made over the tele-
phone yesterday, Mr. Dooman accompanied Messrs. Ukawa and
Okazaki to the Treasury Department to fulfill an engage-
ment at eleven o'clock with Mr. Wayne Taylor, Assistant
Secretary of the Treasury.
After a general exchange of amenities, Mr. Ukawa
stated that he had sought an opportunity to call on a
responsible official of the Treasury Department for the
purpose of giving such information as he had which would
with regard to Japanese exchange policy
be responsive to questions/raised in press accounts of
an interview given to press correspondents by an anonymous
in the interview were, in Mr. Ukawa's opinion, that the
recent Japanese Exchange Control measures were being
carried out with much secrecy, hindering legitimate
business, and were preparing the way for devaluation of the
F/FG
yen.
4
Beginning

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Beginning some time during the early autumn of last
year, Mr. Ukawa continued, speculative imports into Japan
began to increase and reached a point at which it became
difficult to maintain the rate at which the yen had been
stabilized, which was 1s 2d. These speculative transactions
were caused by anticipation that the present session of
the Diet would make substantial changes in the Japanese
tariff. To meet this situation and in order to assure
stabilization of the yen, the Department of Finance had
issued on January 8, 1937, an ordinance requiring the
approval of the Minister of Finance for Foreign Exchange
transactions or for issuance of letters of credit cover-
ing imports. The text of the ordinance was, of course,
made public at the time of promulgation, but Mr. Ukawa
had received a summary of the ordinance which he had had
translated and distributed recently to New York banks
engaged in foreign exchange. He then handed Mr. Taylor
and Mr. Dooman copies of the translation (copy attached).
Mr. Ukawa pointed out that the ordinance is to be effective
only until July 31, 1937, and was, therefore, only a
provisional measure.
Mr. Ukawa then referred to the impression held in
the United States that the Exchange Control measure was
being carried out in such manner as to hinder legitimate
694.116
6
trade. He explained that
the measure was a technical
one and some time would be required before it could be
made

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
made to work smoothly and satisfactorily. The information
he had indicated that the permits required were already
being granted in respect of legitimate commercial trans-
actions.
Mr. Ukawa said that there is no warrant whatever for
the suggestion that the yen is to be devaluated. The
Japanese Government is determined to maintain the exchange
value of the yen at 1s 2d, and that Mr. Ukawa was informed
that the Governor of the Bank of Japan was of the opinion
that, by resort to Exchange Control, the rate could be
maintained.
Mr. Taylor expressed very gracefully his appreciation
for the information which Mr. Ukawa had given him. He
then asked whether the Japanese Government intended to
extend the ordinance of January 8 when it expired on
July 31, 1937, or whether it would be replaced by some
other measure of control. Mr. Ukawa's reply was evasive,
it consisting of a repetition of his previous statement
that the measure is designed to stabilize the yen in
terms of foreign exchange. Mr. Okazaki then intervened and
said that there were at least three factors which made
necessary the enforcement of the present measure, first, antic-
ipated increase in expenditures for naval and military purposes,
second, an increased call on Japanese credits abroad as a
result of the conclusion of the Japanese-Australian trade
arrangement, and, three, speculative imports of merchandise
in

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
in anticipation of increases in the Japanese import tariff.
Mr. Okazaki added that there were probably other factors,
but that there was no doubt that the three factors which
he had mentioned had weakened the exchange position of
the yen, and that the measures under reference were
designed to remove the threat to the yen arising therefrom.
It was obvious, Mr. Okazaki went on to say, that the
circumstances which he had described would preclude the
making of any prediction as to the duration of measures
considered necessary to protect the yen -- that it was
possible that conditions might improve so substantially
as to warrant the lifting before July 31, 1937, of any
control, but that on the other hand if the threat to the
yen persisted the Government would probably continue
after August 1, 1937, the present ordinance, extend the
ordinance, or replace it with legislation of an even
more stringent character.
Mr. Taylor made the comment that the explanation
given by Mr. Okazaki would assist the Treasury Department
in understanding the situation in Japan. He did not, of
course, wish to make any comment with regard to the
measures taken by the Japanese Government, but 
American bankers had shown apprehension over the delay
in the completion of Japanese exchange transactions.
From his knowledge of such matters he could realize that
it would take time to put the Exchange Control into full
effect.
Mr. Ukawa

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
Mr. Ukawa observed at this point that he would
appreciate receiving Mr. Taylor's comment with regard to
the apprehension over the future of the yen expressed by
the Treasury Department in the course of the interview reported
yesterday by the press. Mr. Taylor said that it had recent-
1y become necessary for the Treasury Department to request
of Congress an extension of certain extraordinary powers
vested in the Treasury Department, and that in the course
of an executive meeting of the appropriate committee of
Congress the Treasury Department had put forward, as a
reason for the desired extension of extraordinary powers,
the likelihood of emergency situations arising unexpectedly
in various parts of the world. It so happened at that
time that the Japanese financial situation was being dis-
cussed by press despatches from Tokyo, and questions were
asked by Congressmen with regard to that situation.
Mr. Taylor assumed that there had been some leakage of
statements made at that meeting.
Mr. Taylor then said that he hoped that the contact
which had been established by Mr. Ukawa's call between
the financial authorities of the two Governments would be
maintained. The Treasury Department would be glad to
receive Mr. Ukawa at any time. Mr. Taylor said that every
consideration would be given to requests on the part of
the Japanese Government for information available to the
Treasury Department. Mr. Okazaki stated that he was very
grateful

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
grateful to Mr. Taylor for having received him and
Mr. Ukawa and for the opportunity which had been thus
afforded the Japanese Government to present information
as to its exchange and currency policies. He wondered
whether it would be possible for the Treasury Department
to issue some statement which would be calculated to
allay the apprehension arising out of the reports appear-
ing yesterday in the press. Mr. Dooman suggested that
if the Treasury Department were disposed favorably toward
the request just made by Mr. Okazaki, the announcement
to the press be made very brief but so worded as to convey
the impression to the interested public that the Japanese
Government is not withholding information as to the
operation of those measures. Mr. Taylor replied that he
would be glad to take up with the Secretary of the
Treasury the matter of the issuance of a public statement.
The conversation concluded with further expressions
of appreciation on the part of Messrs. Ukawa and Okazaki
for the courtesy shown them by Mr. Taylor.
Note: Mr. Taylor later in the morning called Mr.
Dooman on the telephone and stated that the Secretary
of the Treasury desired, for certain special reasons,
that the proposed announcement to the press be issued
by the Department of State rather than by the Treasury
Department.

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.88)
-7 -
Department. Mr. Dooman replied that he would draft a
statement for release to the press and would refer the
text by telephone to Mr. Taylor.

The Summary of the Ordinance

of January 8, 1937.
The Department of Finance promulgated the Ordinance of January

the exchange control regulations. The Ordinance was put into
effect on Janu-ry 8, 1937 and is to remain in force until July 31,
1937. The outline of the Ordinance is as follows:

One. Exchange permits are being issued gradually to
cover contracts for business booked before the new regu-
lations came into force. Cotton importers have been
especially favored and it is reported that most of their
contracts up to April shipment from the United States have
been covered with exchange permits. Some importers
of other lines complain that they can obtain no permits for
business previously booked but it is believed that the
delay is due in part at least to the technical difficulties.
The Ministry of Finance is placing new minor restrictions
FILED
upon the granting of permits thereby causing further delay
and giving the government time slowly to bring the actual
as opposed to the nominal exchange value of the yen to one
shilling two pence.
Two. As a result of the exchange control regulations
the exchange market is much firmer than it was before
January 8.

Ministry of Commerce and Industry has announced the quan-

may be imported in l937. It is not believed, however, that
these are strict limitations because such limitations might
restrict Japanese industry and export trade. It is more
probable that the quantities set are intended to act as a
measuring rod to determine the amount of exchange which
must be covered.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
RISEHVEASTERN AFFAIRS
February 11, 1937
-a ey  51
MMH:
DIVISION OF
SKH:
MMUNICATIONS
received in the Division last July was referred to a number
of authorities for comment. The thesis of the STATIST
article is that Japan's export boom is declining while
894.51/484
imports are increasing; the manufacture of arms is thriving,
while depression in those branches of industry which cater
to the home market is becoming more pronounced; the
uncortainties in regard to government policies are causing
894.51
a feeling of suspense in Japanese business circles. In
general, the writer of the STATIST article takes a very
11
pessimistic view of the financial and economic situation
2
in Japan.
I have made some effort to summarize the various views
expressed by commentators on this article, and to draw some
general conclusions therefrom. The common view expressed
in the reports by the Tokyo Embassy and the Department of
FILED
Commerce, and in the economic report on Japan by Sir George
Sansom, recently received in the Division, is that the
89
presence of one factor makes it impossible to make any
R
accurate prediction concerning the future course of Japa-
A
nese finance. This factor is, of course, the policy of
the military -- which is unpredictable.
From

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
2
From a purely economic and commercial standpoint all
the authorities consulted agree that Japan's situation is
not at all dangerous or even unfavorable. Japan's increas-
ing national debt is often pointed to as a danger point.
The national debt, now approximately ten billion yen, repre-
sents a per capita burden of Yen 106.75 or $31.49 (U.S. per
capita debt is approximately $275). It is the Embassy's
view that this debt is not excessive. Other authorities
agree that Japan can stand a considerably greater debt.
Dr. Baba, Finance Minister in the Hirota Cabinet, stated
that according to his calculations of the increase of
national wealth, Japan could absorb further deficit bonds
to the amount of one billion yen per annum. In this
opinion the foreign bankers concur, provided that the rate
of increase of foreign trade does not decline appreciably.
A second danger signal often pointed to by pessimistic
observers is the gradual leveling off of the rate of expan-
sion of the Japanese export trade. The Department of Com-
merce comments that the fact that exports are not leaping
forward at the same rate as in the past two years is not
necessarily a sign of disaster. In fact, it could hardly
have been expected that they should continue to maintain
such a rate of increase. Even if export returns fell short
of the records set in 1934 or 1935, profit still could be
shown. Actually, while the rate of increase of exports is
slowing down the trade is still expanding. Moreover, this
slowing down has occurred before and may be only temporary.
st111

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Still another factor regarded as ominous by observers
is the increase of imports in relation to exports. In this
connection the following quotation from Samson's report,
above mentioned, is pertinent:
"An optimistic outlook would take into considera-
tion that in relation to the total wealth of Japan
or to her national income, .... the debit balance
in international account - Yen 120,000,000 in 1934
and probably less in 1935 - is of no great magni-
tude and would be cancelled by a small increase in
export prices."
As Mr. Dooman pointed out in his memorandum of July 30, 1936,
it is easily possible to exaggerate the importance of the
effect of an unfavorable visible trade balance on national
finance, particularly in the case of a country like Japan
which has a large invisible income from its merchant marine
and from immigrant remittances.
According to some observers still another source of
danger to Japan's economy is the decreased income of the
workers and farmers. Wages are unquestionably low but as
yet there seems not to have occurred any appreciable decline
in real income. On the contrary, the activity in the export
trade, munitions manufacture, and the increase in the prices
of agricultural commodities has bettered the condition of
both factory workers and farmers in the last year or more.
The fact is that the average Japanese worker, agricultural
or industrial, is little or no worse off than he has ever
been.
The

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
The writer in the London STATIST suggests that another
danger lies in the uncertain position of the Japanese yen
and the possibility of further inflation. There is unques-
tionably danger to the finances of the country from this
source, largely as a result of military expenditures. There
has been recent evidence of difficulty in maintaining the
yen's exchange value. However, the following quotation
from a report dated July 6, 1936, from the Trade Commissioner
at Tokyo, is pertinent:
"Personally I can see no evidence of real
inflation or of serious stress to the financial or
economic structure. On the contrary, finance, trade
and industry appear to be sound and healthy at the
present time -- although the future is somewhat
clouded by the certainty of a marked increase in
government expenditures (and consequently in the
issue of deficit bonds), and by Japan's heavy
financial commitments in Manchuria!
To repeat the statement made in the first part of this
memorandum: It is impossible for anyone to predict with
accuracy the course of Japan's finance and economy without
taking into consideration political developments. If the
military continue to follow their program of activity on
the continent, of expansion at home, and of pressing toward
a totalitarian organization of the state, one may confidently
expect drastic effects on the financial and economic organiza-
tion of Japan within the next few years. If, on the other
hand, the program of military activity on the continent is
gradually decreased and if the military are unable to put
into effect at home their ideas of expansion of armament and
of

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.96)
-5-
of reorganization of the state there would appear to be no
reason to fear serious financial or economic difficulties.
The following is quoted from Sir George Sansom's report:
"A slight turn of the wheel of fortune can easily falsify
calculations as to the future course of trade and finance,
and more than once in the past, when the situation looked
unpromising, some unforeseen development has relieved it.
It is therefore better not to prophesy, but to confine
This in a
oneself to pointing out that the present margin of safety
chronie
is rather small." Personally, I feel that in the light of
what Japan has accomplished in the past few years and in
view of the demonstrated sagacity, resourcefulness and
industry of the Japanese people, one need have no fear of a
major economic catastrophe.
2nD
FE:WTT/DLY

FOR THE SECRETARY OF THE TREASURY.
894.51/485
One. The general financial outlook in Japan is much
brighter than it was a month ago due largely to public
confidence in Yuki's policies, the principal one being
that instead of imposing overly heavy burdens of governmental
spending on the inadequate economic structure it is better
first to augment the economic resources in order to care
for the increasing burdens. Consequently the budget has
been decreased somewhat which has checked the too rapid
rise in prices. It is now believed that with the as
FILED
2
sistance of Ikeda and Tsushima, Yuki will be able to slo
161
the Government deficit bonds in l937 (somewhat less
amount than the Baba estimate) without great difficulty
al though he may not be able to convert more outstanding
high interest bonds into low interest issues.
F
Two. Foreign exchange control is operating effectively
FG
and the yen is firm with the actual as well as the nominal
exchange value at one shilling two pence. Permits for
1C
exchange

=== Page 52 === (confidence 1.00)
U -2- #59, Feb. 19, 5 p.m. from Tokyo
exchange to cover import business previously booked are
being granted steadily and some new business is now being
covered. It is believed that Yuki's administration of the
exchange control will be more liberal than Baba's in order
not to interfere with Yuki's policy of fostering industry
and trade.
Three. In reply to interpellations in the Diet yes-
terday Yuki intimated that specie shipments abroad may be
made for the settlement of international trade accounts
and said that such shipments within a limited amount need
cause no uneasiness in regard to Japan's specie reserve.
Some observers consider it possible that Yuki may use a
part of the idle specie reserve which is greatly in ex-
cess of the proportion usually considered necessary to
secure the currency issue to buy up some of the 62%
Japanese dollar bonds floated in the United States.
GREW

REGARDING:
Financial situation in Japan:Reports regarding -,

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The financial situation in Japan during January was
less favorable than it had been for a number of years.*
However, conditions have not as yet become serious and the
51
prevailing opinion seemed to be that the Government would
8
be able to avoid a dangerous crisis for some months and
perhaps through the year 1937.**
There were the following
favorable factors: the national debt, although on the rise,
did not appear to be excessive fornation of the size and
a
power of Japan; money rates were easy; there was little
or no currency inflation; and the unfavorable visible
foreign trade balance for l936 was partly offset by a
favorable invisible trade balance.
On the other hand, there were unfavorable factors,
principally the unbalanced budget and the consequent large
issues of "deficit bonds" necessary in 1937. The weakness..
of the yen and apprehension of the further pressure which
would result from large speculative imports expected in
anticipation of the proposed increased tariff rates were
the causes of the foreign exchange control regulations
announced by the Government and put into force on January 8.
The adverse factors in Japan's financial position were due
in great measure to the unusually heavy governmental ex-
penditures, principally military.
Strong
*
Embassy's telegram No. 5 of January 13, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2209 of January 1, 1937.

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
Strong opposition to the Cabinet's financial policy,
principally to the proposed tax reform, continued to be
expressed. In fact, the widespread dissatisfaction over
the Government's financial policy formed an important part
of the strong opposition to the Government which brought
about the Hirota Cabinet's downfall toward the close of the
month.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan in January amounted to ¥200,304,000
and imports into this country were ¥304,126,000, as compared
with December exports of ¥296,817,000 and imports of ¥284,
515,O00. This represented an unfavorable balance of trade
of ¥l03,822,000, as compared with an unfavorable balance for
December of only ¥12,302,000.
(b). Financial Trends.
The outstanding feature of the month was the sharp rise
in commodity prices, the Bank of Japan's wholesale price
index number for January being 233.3 as against 214.9 for
December 1936. .Bond and stock prices appreciated. In the
latter part of the month the activity of the stock market
decreased owing to the Cabinet crisis. The average yen rate
of the Yokohama Specie Bank was $0.28625 as compared with
$0.28552 for December. It should be noted that the main-
tenance of the yen at a slightly higher level during January
was due to the fact that the exchange was artifioially
bolstered by the Yokohama Specie Bank and that strict
exchange control was enforced.

CONFIDENTIAL
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
OFFICE OF THE ECONOMIC ADVISER
April 20, 1937.
MEMORANDUM
CONCERNING THE FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC ASPECTS
OF THE JAPANESE SITUATION
In November of 1934 there was prepared in this office,
with considerable assistance from the Division of Far
Eastern Affairs, a study of economic and financial condi-
tions in Japan, the object of which was to determine the
ability of the Japanese Government to carry on an exten-
sive program of increased armaments and to continue
extensive military expenditures in "Manchukuo"and North
China. The general conclusions of this study were that
the public finances of Japan were in relatively good
condition, that industrial development was on a solid
basis, and that there appeared to be no reason why Japan
could not finance relatively heavy expenditures if, for
reasons of national policy, she desired to do so. It is
the object of this memorandum to bring the earlier study
up to date and, in the light of developments since
November 1934, to reappraise the situation.
Summary and Conclusions
Internal financing.
The expenditures of the Japanese Government since
1933

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
1933 have continued steadily to increase. During the
fiscal year ended March 31, 1933, the total expenditures
of the general account amounted to 1,950,000,000 yen; in
the fiscal year ended March 31, 1937, expenditures were
about 2,312,000,000 yen, while the budget for 1937-1933
calls for an expenditure of 2,815,000,000 yen, an incrcase
from the preceding year of about 500,000,000 yen.
Revenues have not kept pace with expenditures; they
increased from 1,331,000,000 yen in the fiscal year 1932-
1933, to an estimated figure of 1,s00,000,000 yen in the
fiscal year 1936, while the budget for 1937-1938 is
reported to call for tax increases which are expected to
raise the revenue in that year to about 2,000,000,000 yen.
The continued excess of expenditures over revenues
has naturally necessitated a steady increase in the
national debt. During each of the fiscal years since
March 31, 1933, the Government has issued bonds to a
value of between 700 and 881 million yen. The national
debt has thus increased from about 7 billions of yen on
March 31, 1933, to about 10,500,000,000 yen on March 31,
1937, while the bond issue envisaged by the budget for
1937-1938 wi11 bring the debt to about 11,500,000,000
on March 31, 1938, with a probable deficit of between
750,000,000 and 1,000,000,000 yen to be covered by a bond
issue

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
issue during the succeeding year. Further, a portion of
the national debt is an external debt calculated at the
old gold value of the yen; if calculated at the present
exchange value of the yen, in which interest and princinal
are paid, the debt figures quoted above vould be larger
by one-half billion to one billion yen.
Although the public debt of Japan is large and has
probably increased at a rate excecding the growth of the
productive capacity of the country, the consensus of
opinion among financial observers is that it is not
dangerously large for a country with productive resources
as extensive as those of Japan.
It is obvious that one of the most important factors
in the ability of the Japanose Government to continue its
large military cxponditures is its ability to borrow, and
this dcponds primarily upon the ability and willingness
of the banking system to absorb new issues of bonds. At
the end of 1933, 40 percent and at the end of 1936, 44.5
percent of the total debt was held by banks, with an
additional 16.6 percent and 18.1 percent in each of those
years, respectively, held by the Deposit Bureau of the
Department of Finance. At the end of 1936, Government
bonds constituted 47.4 percent of the investment portfolios
of commercial banks, and about S6 percent and 32 percent
of

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
of the investment portfolios of the savings banks and
trust companies, respectively. These figures do not
indicate the banks to be overloaded with Government bonds.
Presumably the private banks can and will absorb new
issues of Government bonds. Thus far, the Bank of Japan
has experienced little difficulty in disposing of Govern-
ment bonds. Neither its bond holdings nor the note issue
has materially increased; presumably both could be in-
creased to provide an enlarged market for Government bonds.
In spite of the relatively favoraple facts and figures
cited above, the future of Government financing does not
appear to be bright. Competition for credit between the
Government and private industry has manifested itself; this
competition plus the munitions boom, incrcased priccs,
weakness of the exchange value of the yen, and fear of
inflation resulting from announcement of the 1937-1938
budget - these factors operated to drive down the price of
Government bonds below the issue price late in 1936.
Although the situation was temporary, it has becn evi-
denced that the Government oxpects some difficulty in its
financing by its recent decision to conduct open-market
operations through the Bank of Japan and its rccent indi-
cation that it may resort to compulsion on the banks to
dispose of its bonds in 1937.
While

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
While both wholesale and retail prices in Japan have
increascd largely in recent years, mage rates not only
nave showed no tendency to rise but actually shoved slight
declines. This situation presages labor unrest and
eventual wage increases. Strikes and labor difficultics
are increasing in Japan and it seems almost inevitable
that wages shall rise.
From the facts and figurcs vhich are available it is
necessary to conclude that there nro no convincing reasons
why Japan's program of exponditures may not continue to
be financed provided the Govornmont-military continues to
receive the support of the masses, thich, in the last
analysis, bear the burden. Given this support, the Gov-
ernment possesses the moans to coapel the banks to
cooperate, and it seems probnble that mensures of com-
pulsion mny be necessary in the rolatively near future.
But the continuance of the Japanese people to accopt their
burden, to regard it as necessitated by a state of national
emergency and to accept it ungrudgingly, is itself in some
question. Should the people show signs of deserting the
program, whether the Government-military would capitulate,
or whether it vould resort to compulsion or to military
diversion, is not a matter to be predicted.
2.
External financing.
Perhaps the most unsatisfactory nspect of Japan's
econonic

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
economic and financial position is the state of her inter-
national balance of payments. Japan has had a growing
debit in her international balance of payments for the
last two or three years. Her exchange reserves abroad
are somewhat depleted, her merchandise trade showed a
large debit in 1936 and an even larger one is probable
in 1937 unless checked by exchange control. This is due
in large part, first, to the increased prices of
increased imports of raw materials for the increased
manufacture of armaments and for the needs of Japanese
export industries, and secondly, to increased capital
outlays in Manchuria, mostly of a military nature.
The foreign exchange value of the yen declined pre-
cipately in October 1936 and continued weak during the
remainder of the year. It has regained considerable
1937
 m.strength during the first three months of rs as a
result, in large part, of (1) an exchange control system
established on January 8, 1937, and (2) to the exporta-
tion of about 50,000,000 yen in gold and announcement of
a policy of exporting gold to support the yen. The
present Government is committed to maintaining the
present exchange value of the yen.
It is clear that gold shipments offer no permanent
solution. Although considerable quantities can be
exported without unfortunate results, at the present
rate

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
rate of expenditure in Manchuria such a policy could not
be maintained for more than a year or two.
If exchange control is exercised in an effective
manner to cut down the debit in the international account,
it will undoubtedly have repercussions on Japanese
exports, and it is difficult to sec how thc international
account will be materially improved by this method.
The state of Japan's balance of payments, therefore,
clearly exerts pressure on the Government to cut down its
imports of raw materials for armaments manufacture and to
curtail expenditures in Manchuria and North China. But
this pressurc is being successfully resisted and probably
will continue to be resisted. The only possibility that
is foreseen is that future increases in the revenues of
"Manchukuo" may make it feasible to transfer to the
"Manchukuo" treasury a largor share of the bur den of
supporting the Kwantung army.
Clearly, the alternative to retrenchment is control.
The divided Government of Japan may continue to muddle
along for quite a while, depleting gold reserves, con-
trolling more and more rigidly foreign exchange and the
internal cconomy. Too much control and labor unrest
might lead to a dictatorship.
MEMORANDUM

RECFLAPo11 13 1937.

EA
DIVISION OF
Mr. Feis:
In FE's opinion, EA's memorandum of March 27, 1937,
concerning the public finances of Japan is an excellent
piece of work, especially in view of the paucity of
immediately available statistical material and published
critical analyses on the subject.
FE offers the following suggestions and comnents in
894.51/492
regard to the memorandum for EA's consideration:
At the outset, there appears the statement, "In
November of 1934 there was prepared in this office a study
of economic and financial conditions in Japan. . . ."
Toward giving an accurate accounting, this should, in FE's
opinion, read in effect, "In November of 1934 there was
prepared in this office, with considerable assistance from
the Division of Far Eastern Affairs, a study of economic
and financial conditions in Japan. . . ."!
With regard to the discussion of wages, in numbered
paragraph 2 on page 19, and of wages and prices, in para-
graph (f) on page 31, inasmuch as rising prices and wages
equally affect production in other countries besides Japan,
it is thought that too much emphasis should not be placed
upon them as factors in curtailing Japan's export trade.
Furthermore, there is probably further room for rationaliza-
F
tion of industry, which would reduce the cost of manufaoture
1
B
and

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
and thus offset rising costs of raw materials and higher
wages.
On page 32 in numbered paragraph 1 in connection
with the suggestion that curtailment of military expenditures
in North China and Manchuria, possibly accompanied by a
decrease in military expenditure at home, would be the
simplest measure of relief, it is stated that the "former
is more plausible than the latter, which is scarcely worth
mentioning". The only possibility in this direction that
K
is foreseen is that future increases in the revenues of
"Manchukuo" may make it feasible to transfer to the
"Manchukuo" treasury a larger share of the burden now
being borne by the Japanese Treasury for the upkeep of the
Kwantung Army.
Japan's ability to absorb deficit bonds, which will
amount to about Yen 752,000,000 during the current fiscal
year, is largely dependent upon a continuation of the
present industrial prosperity. This prosperity may be
adversely affected by increased taxation which has been
Yeot
projected. It would be premature to discuss this phase
of the subject, as detailed information is not yet available,
but it is thought that this point should be mentioned as
a factor in the future situation.
At the end of the memorandum (pp. 29-32), there is a
statement of "conclusions". The separate items in this
statement are very interesting, but it is believed desirable
that an effort be made, by way of formulating a conclusion
to

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to conclusions, to offer some concise statement to the
effect that there is or is not warrant for believing that
Japan's political efforts are on the verge of a breakdown
because of the overburden which they impose upon Japan's
financial and economic capacities.
It would seem advisable also to include in the survey
of Japan's financial position a statement to the following
effect: Although an examination of the economic data
available would indicate that there is no reason for
assuming that Japan cannot continue to finance relatively
heavy expenditures for military purposes, there are so
many imponderables and uncertainties, as well as factors
other than economic, which enter into the situation that
it would be unwise to adhere unqualifiedly to the contrary
assumption. There would appear to be no warrant for any
assumption that Japan is using up capital reserves in her
struggle for markets abroad. However, it is generally
agreed that Japan's financial position imposes a heavy
burden of sacrifice upon the public, especially the masses.
It may be questioned how long the Japanese public will
continue to regard this burden as being necessitated by a
state of national emergency and to accept it ungrudgingly,
and also whether the vitality of the nation's man-power,
its chief industrial asset, is not being so impaired by
the strain as to prejudice the nation's future productive
capacity.

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
capacity. Internal political and social unrest, operating
directly as well as indirectly by tempting the military
group to seek a way out through creating complications in
Japan's external relations, are even more likely than
purely economic factors to develop so as to affect Japan's
industrial progress adversely and to bring about collapse
of the financial program.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
OFFICE OF THE ECONOMIC ADVISER
April 20, 1937.
MEMORANDUM
CONCERNING THE FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC ASPECTS
OF THE JAPANESE SITUATION
In November of 1934 there was prepared in this office,
with considerable assistance from the Division of Far
Eastern Affairs, a study of economic and financial condi-
tions in Japan, the object of which was to determine the
ability of the Japanese Government to carry on an exten-
sive program of increased armaments and to continue
extensive military expenditures in "Manchukuo"and North
China. The general conclusions of this study were that
the public finances of Japan were in relatively Sood
condition, that industrial development was on a solid
basis, and that there appeared to be no reason why Japan
could not finance relatively heavy expenditures if, for
reasons of national policy, she desired to do so. It is
the objoct of this memorandum to bring the earlier study
up to date and, in the light of developments since
November 1934, to reappraise the situation.
Summary and Conclusions
1.
Internal financing.
The expenditures of the Japanese Government since
1933

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
1933 have continued steadily to increase. During the
fiscal year ended March 31, 1933, the total expenditures
of the scneral account amounted to 1,950,000,000 yen; in
the fiscal year ended March 31, 1937, expenditures were
about 2,312,000,000 yen, whil the budget for 1937-1938
cal1s for an expenditure of 2,815,000,000 yen, an incronse
from the preceding year of about 500,000,000 yen.
Revenues have not kept pace with expenditures; they
increased from 1,331,000,000 yen in the fiscal year 1932-
1933, to an estimated figure of 1,s00,000,000 yen in the
fiscal year 1936, while the budget for 1937-1938 is
reported to call for tax increases which are expected to
raise the revenue in that year to about 2,000,000, 000 yen.
The continued excess of expenditures over revenues
has naturally necessitated a steady increase in the
national debt. During each of the fiscal years since
March 31, 1933, the Government has issued bonds to a
value of between 700 and 881 million yen. The national
debt has thus increased from about 7 billions of yen on
March 31, 1933, to about 10,500,000,000 yen on March 31,
1937, while the bond issue envisaged by the budget for
1937-1938 wi11 bring the debt to about 11,500,000,000
on March 31, 1938, with a probablo deficit of between
750,000,000 and 1,000,000,000 yen to be covered by a bond
issue

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
issue during the succeeding year. Further, a portion of
the national debt is an external debt calculated at the
old gold value of the yen; if calculated at the present
exchange value of the yen, in which intcrest and princinal
are paid, the debt figures quoted above would be larger
2
by one-half billion to one billion yen.
Although the public debt of Japan is large and has
probably increased at a rate excecding the growth of the
productive capacity of the country, the consensus of
opinion among financial observers is that it is not
dangerously large for a country with productive resources
as extensive as those of Japan.
It is obvious that one of tho most important factors
in the ability of the Japanese Government to continue its
large military cxponditures is its ability to borrow, and
this doponds primarily upon the ability and willingness
of the banking system to absorb new issues of bonds. At
the end of 1933, 40 percent and at the end of 1936, 44.5
percent of the total debt was held by banks, with an
additional 16.6 percent and 18.1 percent in each of those
years, respectively, held by the Deposit Bureau of the
Department of Finance. At the end of 1936, Government
bonds constituted 47.4 percent of thc investment portfolios
of commercial banks, and about 66 percent and 32 percent
of

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.97)
-4
of the investment portfolios of the savings banks and
trust companics, respectively. These figures do not
indicate the banks to be overloaded with Government bonds.
Presumably the private banks can and will aosorb now
issues of Government bonds. Thus far, the Bank of Japan
has experienced little difficulty in disposing of Govern-
ment bonds. Neither its bond holdings nor the note issue
has materially increased; presumably both could be in-
creased to provide an enlarged market for Government bonds.
In spite of the relatively favorable facts and figures
cited above, the future of Governmont financing does not
appear to be bright. Competition for credit between the
Government and private industry has manifested itself; this
competition plus the munitions boos, incrcased priccs,
weakness of the exchange value of the yen, and fear of
inflation resulting from announcement of the 1937-1938
budget - these factors operated to drive dom the price of
Government bonds below the issue price late in 1936.
Although the situation was temporary, it has been evi-
denced that the Government oxpects some difficulty in its
financing by its recent dccision to conduct open-market
●
operations through the Bank of Japan and its recent indi-
cation that it may resort to compulsion on the banks to
dispose of its bonds in 1937.
While

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.97)
-5-
While both wholesalo and retail prices in Japan have
increased largely in recent years, mage rates not only
nave shored no tondenoy to rise but actually shomed slight
declines. This situation presages labor unrest and
eventual wage increases. Strikes and labor difficultics
7
are increasing in Japan and it seems almost inevitable
that wages shall rise.
From tho facts and figures vhich are available it is
necessary to conoludo that there nre no convincing reasons
why Japan's program of exponditures may not continue to
be financed provided the Governmont-military continues to
receive the support of the
mosses, which, in the last
analysis, bear the burden.
Givon this support, the Gov-
ernment possesses the aonns to compel the banks to
cooperate, and it seems probable that mensures of com-
pulsion nny be necessary in the rulatively near future.
But the continuance of the Japanese people to accopt their
burden, to regard it as necessitated by a state of national
omergenoy and to nccept it ungrudgingly, is itself in somo
question. Should the people sho signs of deserting the
0
progran, whether the Government-military vould capitulate,
or whether it would rosort to compulsion or to military
diversion, is not a matter to be predicted.
2.
External financing.
Perhaps the most unsatisfactory aspect of Japan's
econonic

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
economic and financial position is the state of her inter-
national balance of payments. Japan has had a growing
debit in her international balance of payments for the
last two or three years. Her exchange reserves abroad
are somewhat depleted, her merchandise trade showed a
large debit in 1936 and an even larger one is probable
in l937 unless checked by exchange control. This is due
in large part, first, to the increased prices of
increased imports of raw materials for the increased
manufacture of armaments and for the needs of Japanese
export industries, and secondly, to increased capital
outlays in Manchuria, mostly of a military nature.
The foreign exchange value of the yen declined pre-
cipately in October 1936 and continued weak during the
remainder of the year. It has regained considerable
strength during the first three months of 193s as a
result, in large part, of (1) an exchange control system
established on January &, 1937, and (2) to the exporta-
tion of about 50,000,000 yen in gold and announcement of
a policy of exporting gold to support the yen.
Tne
present Government is committed to maintaining the
present exchange value of the yen.
It is clear that old shipments offer no permanent
solution. Although considerable quantities can be
exported without unfortunate results, at the present
rate

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
rate of expenditure in Manchuria such a policy could not
be maintained for more than a year or two.
If exchange control is exercised in an offective
manner to cut down the debit in the international account,
it will undoubtedly have repercussions on Japanese
exports, and it is difficult to sec how the international
account will be materially improved by this method.
The state of Japan's balance of payments, therefore,
clearly exerts pressure on the Govornment to cut down its
imports of raw materials for armamonts manufacture and to
curtail oxpenditures in Manchuria and North China. But
this pressurc is being successfully resisted and vrobably
will continue to be resisted.
. The only possibility that
is foreseen is that future increases in the revenues of
"Manchukuo" may make it feasible to transfer to the
"Manchukuo" treasury a largor share of the bur den of
supporting the Kwantung army.
Clearly, the alternative to retrenchment is control.
The divided Government of Japan may continue to muddle
along for quite a while, depleting gold reserves, con-
trolling more and more rigidly foreign exchange and the
internal economy. Too much control and labor unrest
might lead to a dictatorship.

Production and price indices, wage and exchange rates,
et cetera.
In the following tables are shovn various indications
of the present condition of the Japanese economy. The
volume of industrial production increased about 79 percent
between 1931 and 1935, remained relatively stationary
during the first six months of 1936, and then advanced
sharply durine the latter months of that year.
Retail prices, though showing no such drastic in-
crease, showed considerable rise from 146 in 1933 to
about 159 in 1936, suddenly increasing to 163 in Decem-
ber of the latter year.
Wage rates, however, not only showed no tendency to
rise but actually showed slight declines.
Wholesale prices in Japan fell from 100 in 1929 to a
low

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
-23-
low of 69.6 in 1931 and incrcased thereafter to 84.4 in
1935, while wholesale prices in the United States reached
their 1ow of 68 in 1932 (1929 = 100), rose to 69.3 in
1933, and increased to about 84 in 1935, about the same
level as in Japan. But during 1936, prices increased
much more rapidly in Japan; and in Deceuber of 1936 and
January 1937, prices in Japan rose sharply to about 10?
during the latter month. Recent reports from Tokyo
indicate a slight decline in average prices in February.
During 1936, and particularly in December 1936 and
January 1937, wholesale prices in the United States
showed decided increases, but in January 1937 were only
at the figure of 89.4.
The exchange value of the yen, both in terms of the
dollar and the pound sterling, was strong in 1933 until
October, when it slumped decidedly. In terms of dollars
the yen has remained weak since that time, but in terms
of the pound sterline it recovered considerably in
January, and recont reports (March and April) indicate
it to be considerably stronger. The yon is unofficially
pegged to the pound sterling; the Government has attempted
to keep the yen at 1s 3d, and is nov committed to maintain
that rate; the recovery of the yen in terms of pound
sterling is due at least in part to careful bolstering
operati crs on the part of tho Government.

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)

Certain implications of those tables require comment:
1. The relatively larger incrcase in priccs in Japan
in recent months than in the United States appears to have
been brought about at least in part by a wave of speculative
buying. This was in part a fcature of the world-vide
munitions boom, but was probably carried to excess in
Japan because of the fear of inflation resulting from
announcement of Finance Minister Baba's proposed budget
for 1937-1938 with its greatly increased expenditures
and inevitable new indebtednoss. To the extent that
prices in Japan remain above world prices, Japan's
competitivo position in morld markets will be loss
favorable, with obvious implications on the balance of
payments.
2. Unusually heavy imports of rav materials are
expected in 1937 in order to carry out the armaments
progran and to provido for the needs of industry; valued
at higher prices these imports will bear heavily on the
international account. Foroign trado returns for the
first tro months of 1937 indicate imports to be increas-
ing at a greater rate than exports.
3. The failure of wages to increase vith prices
gives promise of labor difficulties. Our Commercial
Attachó in Tokyo (No. 34, February 23, 1937) reports
demands for wage increases to be increasingly numerous
and

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.97)
-25-
and urgent, and that it is only a question of time until
labor unrest vill force wage increases. Moanvhile, the
increasing prices of rav silk and rice have improved the
situation among the farmers to such an oxtent that the
pressure of farm labor on factory labor is easing; the
factory laborer, therefore, may oxpect less difficulty
in achieving higher vagos.
. Wage increnscs naturally vill
tend to increase export prices; hovever, in vier of the
world-vide increase in vages and prices, increases in
Japan may not necessarily oporato to curtail cxports.
International balance of payments.
Perhaps the most unsatisfactory aspect of Japan's
cconomic and financial position is the state of her
international balance of payments. It appeared in 1935
as though Japan was to acquire a credit balance in her
merchandiso trade; the adverse morehandise trade balance
was cut down to about 15 millions of yen in that year.
But in 1936, due partly to incrcased ra- material prices
and partly to the advance in the manufacture of armaments,
the adverse merchandise balance increased to lz0 millions
of yen in 1936, and an even laryer adverse balance is
predicted for the year 1937 unless exchange restrictions
operate severely to rostrict imports. (Seo belou for a
more detailed discussion of these restrictions and their
operation.)

Japan usually has a substantial credit balancc as
regards shipping, insurance, and other such international
services. In 1935 this credit balance vas about 178
millions of yen. It will probably be cubstantially
greater in 1936.
Japan usually has a large dobit balance in capital
movements. In 1935 this debit balance increased to 372
millions of yen as a result of military outlays in
Manchuria and redemption of 61 million yen of South
Manchuria Railway sterling debentures. Although the
Yokohame. Specie Bank's advance estimates indicate little
if any debit as regards capital movements in l936, the
London Statist estimates suc: debit to be at least 400
millions of yen.
The truth is somewhere in between these
tro ostimates.
At this point it mny be noted that a largo part of
the capital transfers from Japan to "Manchukuo" are more
in the nature of gifts or military expenditures than
investments; therefore, returns on a large part of the
"investment" in the form of dividends and interest may
not be expected in the future to aid Japan's inter-
national balance.
In 1935 Japan had a nut credit balance in specic

vill not be available to balance her 1936 accounts, for

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)

occasioned the announcoment of two protective measures:
(1) a greater measure of control of foreign oxchange by
the Government, and (2) the exportation of gold.
The new exchange control ordinance requires Govern-
mont pormits or licenses for all exchange transactions
covering the settlement of accounts for imports, the
acquisition of letters of credit, the disposal or
utilization of securities held abroad, and borroving
abroad in foreign currencies for the purpose of financ-
ing imports. The ordinance vas made retroactive to cover
exchange contracts entered into prior to January 8.
Horever, exchange contracts covering import orders for
less than 30,000 yen monthly are excluded.
Appa.rent ly
contracts for imports already landed or in transit prior
to January 8 are also excluded. The ordinance prohibits
the use of discount bills to finance imports; this pro-
vision applies to goods shipped after one week from the
effective date of the ordinance. Foreign exchange banks
are required to report all exchange transactions and
letters of credit issued.
Permission is necessary for
foreign exchange banks to handle remittances of checks,
bills and orders of paynent in amounts exceoding 10,000
yen. All importers who imported goods valued in excess
of 500, 000 yen during 1936 :ust submit reports on the
status of their accounts ored abroad.
The

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.98)
-35-
The exchange ordinance mas terned by the Government
as an emergency measure designed to check the lowering of
the yen exchange and to curb speculative import orders.
The effects these regulations will have on the export
and iuport trade are not clear. The imposition of the new
rogulations constitutes, at least for the time being, a
distinctly disturbing factor to the import trade. Appli-
cations for exchangc permits are being granted slo-ly,
and traders are oxperiencing great difficulty in formulat-
ing plans for the future and in placing orders to cover
their legitimate present and future requiroments. Short-
ages are reported in the stocks of many commodities,
particularly cotton, vool, hoavy oils, and high grado
machinery and tools. Several trado organizations have
filed ener otic protests with the Governnent against the
delay in the issuance of oxchange permits. Importers of
cotton, wool, and pulp are in the midst of a fight vith
Government authorities ho vont to restrict their
purchases.
During February 1937 it vas announced that Japan
would shortly make gold shipnents abroad in order to
replenish her overseas funds and strengthen the exchange
valuc of the yen. Such shipnents during April are
expected to be about 50,000,000 yen.

On April 20, 1937, the Japanese Minister of Finance,
Mr. Toyotaro Yuki, delivered to representatives of the bank

Tokyo, an address of important content. The address was the
94.51/490
subject of the Embassy's telegram No. 116, April 21, 6 p.m.
JUN 80 1937
FTLED
The Finance Minister declared to the bankers that:
(1) In the present juncture of domestic and
foreign affairs, perfection of the nation's defenses is
AC/
imperative.
ANT
5
(2) The civilian ministries also place large finan-
ASSV
pial demands upon the government.
(3) The inevitable large sise of the budget
shouta be faced with full recognition of the urgenoy of
the measures which it necessitates.
(4)

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(4) To bear a huge budget a country must have
a huge production.
(5) From this it is obvious that for Japan economy
in expenditure is vital.
(6) The real remedy for rising prices is increased
production.
(7) Efficient production is large-scale production,
requiring large capital outlay and large output.
(8) Because Japan's raw material resources are few,
a wise importation policy is important.
(9) The profit motive is not, from the national
point of view, a sufficient guide to industry.
(10) The import exchange control which the Govern-
ment has enforced since January 1937 aimed at checking
speculative imports.
(11) This is not sufficient; the government must
extend the scope of this control to the point of favor-
ing certain imports on grounds of industrial necessity
and national interest, and restricting others.
(12) Manchuria is increasingly dependent on Japanese
financing.
(13) Cooperation to encourage investing in China
is urgently needed.
(14) The Government contemplates no radical inno-
vation of control of the banking institutions of the
country, but the bankers should voluntarily adjust their
investing to the national policy, and should not divert
useful funds to speculation in stocks.
A

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
A drop in prices on the stock exchange followed this
address, and Mr. Yuki's statements have been the subject
of widespread comment in the press. Editorial opinion has
been generally favorable. The tone of frankness, so different
from the Prime Minister's generalities on policy, is in it-
self welcomed. The Finance Minister is apparently educating
the business public to the necessity of greater import control
as a protection to Japan's monetary system, at the same time
pointing to the current need for money in a number of fields
in which the Government hopes for private investing. And it
appears that he has a good following. The public likes his
candid, concrete approach to an admittedly difficult problem.
1/
Herewith is enclosed a clipping of a newspaper article
reporting the Finance Minister's speech. In preparing this
despatch the Embassy has also made use of the report contained
in the ASAHI.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Fudasental Financial Policy
of Hayashi Cabinet Outlined
by Finance Minister Yuki.
Policy Determined at Special Cabinet Meeting.
Tokyo, April 3 (Domei)-A special cabinet mseting was held yesterday
at 2:30 in the afternoon at the of: icial residence of the Presier, where Pi-
nenee Minister Yuki outlined his financial policy and secured the approval of
the other ministers present.
Rough Outline of Fundamental Policy.
Basis of Appropriation.
For national welfare and for national security wich necessitated ex-
pansion of armaments, an increased budget was unavoidable. For the nation to
sttstand the added burden, steps must be taken to promot the increase of pro-
duction capacity and expansion of trade. In other words, the udget should be
in direct proportion to the nation's capacity to pay.
894.51/499
Adjustment of Finances.
As early as possible, means must be considered for reorganizing cen-
tral and related provincial financial conditions.
Hatlomel Debt. Since there is no immediate necessity for reorganising
the national debt situation, no steps need be taken either legally or adminis-
tratively.
oney Rate. Lower interest rates are desired, but there is no inten-
tion of bringing this about through force.
Srchange. Roughly, the present exciange rate with England, of ll shil-
linge 2 pence, will be maintained.
Bx ort and Ispart Trade. At the present time it is inpossible to avoid
exsess of imports over exports, but with the expa sion of national production, it
is hoped that the people will voluntarily buy only the very necessary imported
articles, thus eveatually bringing about a favorable trade balance.
hipments of Gold.
The shipment of species abroad will continue through necessity in the fu-
ture, but precaution will be taken to see that the currency will not be thereby
devalnated.
Cost of Living.
The high cost of living brought about through international conditions, of
sourss, eanmot be avoided, but every effort will be expended to reduce high costs
t regalate the relation beteeen supply and desand.
F
Matiemel wealth will be expaaded as mach as pessible to sret the espected
mee tn mupply and production.

By Imperial Ordinance (enclosure 1) of March 30,
1937, the Japanese Government promulgated its budget
for the 1937-1938 fiscal year. It had been approved by
the House of Peers on March 8 and by the House of Repre-
F/AA
sentatives on March 29. The Japanese fiscal year begins
on April 1 and ends on March 31, and consequently this
1-7/
budget is now in effect. The enclosures herewith comprise
a

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
a compilation made by the Embassy from the Japanese text.
The general budget (enclosure 2) authorizes expendi-
tures to a total of ¥ 2,813,937,971, and the supplementary
budget and other accounts swell that figure to approximately
three billion yen. General budget totals for recent years
are as follows. (the first three being settled account figures,
not budget estimates):
1931-1932
1,476 million yen

This year's figure is a 22 per cent increase over last
year's.
Although the general budget is balanced at ¥2,813,937,971,
the revenue side is brought up to that figure only by auth-
orization of public loans in the sum of ¥770,823,677. This
is 27 per cent of the total general budget.
The general budget includes most items of current ex-
penditure. It is divided, both on the revenue and dis-
bursement sides, into ordinary and extraordinary items.
Those that go on repeating year after year are ordinary,
those that might be expected not to are extraordinary,
in theory. Other accounts provide for other projectsr
N
FOR
long-time investment value. In origin, at least, some
suoh distinctions governed; but at the present time it
often difficult to explain on logical grounds the presenee
of a particular item in a given category. Concealment
perhaps one motive for the confusion.
The

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The supplementary budget (enclosure 3) probably grew
out of the use of revenue which was considered in some way
separate from other revenue, but at the present time it has
no particular significance. This year's supplementary
budget amounts to ¥ 58,197,902; of that 89 per cent or
¥ 51,627,902 is to be financed by borrowing.
The extra budget (enclosure 4) is, practically through-
out, authorization for projects which will require disburse-
ments over a period of years. The extra budget authorizes
the Department of Finance to make contracts, outside of and
in addition to the general and supplementary budgets, for the
projects stipulated. Where a stated payment is made during
the year 1937-1938 under such contracts, this year's total
of ¥ 2,813,937,971 plus ¥ 58,197,902 wil1 be thereby augmented.
The creation of credit by some means, to cover the addition-
al amount, is to be expected.
Water works, river improvements, and the like
legitimately under this method of accounting; but some of
BLICATION
this year's items in the extra budget are not so sound.
For example (page 5, enclosure 4), the army is authorized
by the extra budget to contract to pay in the succeeding
year for some ¥ 26,000,000 of ordinary military supplies
and construction. Most of the appropriations stipulate
payment over a number of years, as for example in sub-
sidies, and either allow or specifically call for a first
payment during this year. The disbursements which the
extra budget specifically designates to be made in this
year amount to approximately ¥ 42,000,000; others are
permissively authorized for the present fiscal year.
The

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The table of continuing expenditure (enclosure 5)
is a revision of the accounts of various projects com-
menced in the past and continuing over a period of years.
In this table the budget estimates of earlier years are
year by year displaced by the exact closed account figures
as they become known; new legislation affecting future
disbursement schedules is also incorporated. The table is
a recapitulation, useful for reference, of long-time costs
of particular projects. It is not in itself legislative
authority for current expenditures.
The special accounts budget (enclosure 6) governs
certain accounts each of which is (or attempts to be)
self-balancing, with some source of income other than
government appropriations (e.g., the tobacco monopoly,
government insurance, the government railways). Sub-
ordinate to the special accounts budget is the table
(enclosure 7) of continuing expenditures relating to the
contained special accounts, constructed as a recapitula-
tion, similarly to the above mentioned enclosure 5.
In this year's budget of the various special ac-
counts it is to be observed that, to balance three of them
(page 4), the government is advancing ¥ 143,000,000
through public loans. Adding together the items of the
1937-l938 budget which are authorized to be financed by
borrowing we therefore have:

IMPERIAL ORDINANCLy at Tokye:
Relating to the Budget Estimate

"We sanction the general budget estimate of revenue
and expenditure for the fiscal year 1937-38 and the special

fiscal year, which were approved by the Imperial Diet,
and cause the same promulgated herewith."

BUDGET.
Article 1. The total amount of revenue and expenditure
for the fiscal year 1937-38 is fixed as ¥2,813,937,971
each. The amount of each item of revenue and expendi-
ture shall be as apportioned in the budget esti-
mate (annexed Table A).
Article 2. The items of expenditure mentioned in the
annexed Table B (Continuing Expenditure) are
those to be disbursed in and after the fiscal year
1937-38 as continuing expenditure. In some of those
items the total amount of expenditure previously
fixed and the amount of yearly disbursement are re-
vised or reassessed.
Article 3.

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Article 3. In respect of the items of expenditure
mentioned in the annexed Table C, which are in-
cluded in the aforementioned expenditure estimate,
the balance of the expenditure remaining on hand at
the end of the current fiscal year may be carried over
for use in the ensuing fiscal year, i.e. 1938-39 fiscal
year.
Article 4. The highest amount(meximum limit) of the
Treasury Notes to be issued and borrowings to be made
in accordance with the rules of Article 6 of the Law
of Account shall be ¥500,000,000 in al1 for the
1937-38 fiscal year.
Article 5. The amount of expenditure for which an agree-
ment running over the current and the ensuing fiscal
year may be made according to the rule of Article
II of the Law of Account shall be ¥4,000,000 for
the 1937-38 fiscal year.

BUDGET FOR FISCAL YEAR 1937-1938.
The general budget estimate for the fiscal year
1937-38 (Apri1 1, 1937, to March 31, 1938) as approved
by the Imperial Diet at the last session amounts to
¥2,813,937,971, in both revenue and expenditure,
adding to whioh the supplementary appropriations
amounting to ¥58,197,902 for each the total comes up
to ¥2,872,135,873. The aforementioned figures are all
for general account only, and do not cover the special
account (or special budget). (For details of the budget
figures see the table given elsewhere, in which are
given figures for each item of revenue and expenditure;
The revenue is composed of ordinary revenue amounting
to ¥1,814,696,044 and extraordinary revenue amounting to
999,241,927. Again the expenditure is divided into
ordinary expenditure and extraordinary expenditure
amounting to ¥1,462,712,034 and ¥1,351,225,937 respectively,
or a total of ¥2,813,937,971. Compared with the previous
year's budget it shows an increase of ¥292,125,493 in
revenue and that of ¥402,497,562 in expenditure (a gain
of ¥466,561,678 and ¥502,420,684 respectively when com-
pared with the actual working budget of last year), as
shown below in a tabular form:---

Note:--
The general budget estimate for the fiscal year l937-38,
originally framed at the hand of the Hirota cabinet (which re-
signed on January 23, 1937) and presented to the Imperial Diet
(70th session), was withdrawn by the Government on February 3,
upon the coming to power of the succeeding ministry (Hayashi
cabinet). The budget was then again presented to the Diet by
the new ministry on February 15. The budget received amend-
ments at the hand of the new ministry, the amount thereof
having been reduced to ¥2,813,937,971 for both revenue and
expenditure as against ¥3,041,000,000 each of the original
estimate. The emended budget was approved by the House of
Representatives on March 8 and then by the House of Peers om
March 29, it having been made public in an extra issue of
the official gazette on March 30. Appended is a table showing

LOAN PROJECTS
(Compiled from newspaper sources.)
The amount of prospective loans to be issued during
the next fiscal year commencing April 1 under the revised bud-
get estimate is figured at ¥772,253,000 for general account
and ¥143,000,000 for special account, or a total of ¥915,-
253,000. Compared with the loan project as under the
original budget estimate framed at the hand of the prev-
ious ministry (Finance Minister Mr. Baba), the above figures
show a decrease of ¥30,198,000 in general account and ¥12,-
000,000 in special account, or a decrease of ¥42,198,000 in
the total. Divided into general account and special ac-
count the details are as follows:----
Revised
Original
estimate

In addition to the above total a sum of 3o million yen
is to be raised by means of loan, this amount being requir-
ed for the purpose of balancing prefectural finances as
additional grants-in-aid to local governments, for which
there is no corresponding revenue. The total amount of new
loans to be issued next fiscal year will thus come up to
about ¥945,253,000.

SUPPLEMENTARY BUDGET ESTIMATE (General Account)
The amount of the supplementary budget estimate for the
fiscal year 1937-38 is fixed as ¥58,197,902 each of revenue
and expenditure. The amount of each item of revenue and ex-
penditure is as shown in the annexed table.

Special Account Budget.
The supplementary Budget estimate (both revenue and expen-
diture) for special account for the l937-38 fiscal year mentioned
hereinunder comprises those for the National Debt Amortisation
Fund, National Loans and the Kwantung Bureau,all belonging to
the jurisdiction of the Finence Department; the Imperial Univ-
ersities and Government Universities both belonging to the juris-
diotion of the Education Department; and the Chosen Government-
General belonging to the jurisdiction of the Department of Over-
seas Affairs. The amount of revenue and expenditure for each item
is shown in the budget table appended hereunder. (See Table I)
In respect of the continuing expenditures mentioned in Table 2
given elsewhere the previously fixed total amount as well as the
amount of yearly allotment has been revised or reassessed.
In respect of those items of expenditure mentioned in Table 3
given elsewhere the balance of funds remaining on hand at the
end of the current fiscal year, if any, may be carried forward
for use in the ensuing fiscal year (1938-39 fiscal year)
(N.B. Table 3 referred to in the foregoing paragraph is omitted)

EXTRA BUDGET FOR 1937-38FISUAL TRAK
General Account
Department of Foreign Affairs.
Rental of land and buildings
for Foreign Missions.
Concerning the rental of land and buildings for foreign
missions (Embassies, Legations,etc.), the amount thereof as
under the existing contract may be reassessed, and new
contracts for their lease may be made or old contracts
may be renewed by fixing proper term of lease end annual
amount of rental, the total amount not exceeding ¥1,669,551
a year.
Department of Home Affairs.
Subsidies to Water-works expenditure.
The Department may make a contract with the Treasury for
an outlay to be borne by the Treasury apart from the expendi-
ture included in the general budget estimate on account of
subsidiary grants for water-works expenditure for Muroran City,
parts of Hyogo prefecture, parts of Niigata prefecture, parts of
Fukushima prefecture and part of Kagoshima prefecture, and also
for sewerage works expenditure fo the cities of Nishinomiya,
Shizuoka, Fukuoka and Naha. The total amount of the said sub-
sidies is fixed as ¥1,945, 000 which is to be disbursed in 25
fisoal years beginning l937-38 fiscal year, the amount of yearly
disbursement varying according to rate of progress of the
undertaking.
Subsidy to Ube Port Harbor Works
The Department may make a contract with the Treasury
for an outlay to be borne by the Treasury outside the expen-
diture included in the general budget estimate on account of the
grant of a subsidy to the expenditufor Ube Port harbor works.
The total amount of such subsidy is fixed as 1,589,900 which is
to be disbursed in 8 fiscal years beginning 1937-38 fiscal year.

The Finance Department.
Grants-in-aid for Payment of Interest on
Loans advanced for shipbuilding purposes
and Indemnifications for losses culminating
therefrom.
The Department is authorized to make contracts with those
who accommodate others with necessary funds for shipbuilding pur-
poses to confer upon them grents-in-aid for the payment of inter-
est on such loans and also to allow indemnifications for the losses
they may sustain on account of such loans, on the terms and con-
ditions specified separately, the amount of such loans being,
however, limited to ¥70,000,000, with the rate of interest there-
of fixed at 3.7 per annum.
The Army Department.
Clothing and provisions expenditure;
Ordnance and Military Horse expenditure;
Military transport expenditure;
Fodder and ration expenditure;
Construction expenditure.

stationed in Manchuria);
¥12,000,000 for construction.
