In despatch No. 2513, July 24, 1937, the Embassy
0011  1937
yeer April 1, 1937 to March 31, 1938, and in the para-
GELLE
griph at the top of page three, referred to a supple-
mentary budget which was passed at the same time for
certain purposes. I now have the honor to report upon
a second, a third, and a fourth supplementary budget, all
F/HC
of which have been presented to the Japanese Diet now in
session.
The second supplementary budget, approved by the
House of Representatives on August 3, 1937, appropriates
¥13,380,000,

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
¥13,380,000 from surplus funds of the last fiscal year
for miscellaneous purposes and general account. Its
largest item is one of ten million yen to replenish
the second reserve fund which was depleted by being used
for the first epxenses in connection with the North China
incident of July l937, immediately after the emergency
arose.
The third supplementary budget appropriates seven mil-
lion yen to various general budget accounts, including
seven hundred thousand yen to set up a new department of
health. It also designates a disposition of the book pro-
fits obtained from the recent revaluation of the Government's
gold reserves (Embassy's telegram No. 215, July 21, 4 p.m.).
The third supplementary budget was also passed by the House
of Representatives on August 3.
The fourth supplementary budget has not yet been passed
by either House of the Diet but it is expected to pass in
the form in which it has been submitted by the Cabinet. It
is entirely devoted to expenditures connected with the North
China incident which commenced in July. Newspaper reports
state that it allots ¥257,000,000 to the War Department,
¥95,000,000 to the Navy Department, ¥63,400,000 to the De-
partment of Finance, and ¥4,100,000 to the Foreign Office.
1/2
Newspaper articles covering these supplementary budgets
are enclosed herewith.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AUG 2 8 1937
Subject: Loans by American Banks to the
894.51/505
Yokohama Specie Bank.
Mr. Harrison called me on the telephone; said that
he had been trying to get the Secretary; and said that
there was certain information which he would like to
have conveyed to the Secretary.
894.51
Mr. Harrison said that New York banks have been
making loans to the Yokohama Specie Bank on security of
gold in transit and of U.S. bonds. They have received
word today from London that London banks, which previously
have been making suoh loans, have today discontinued
FILED
making loans to the Yokohama Specie Bank (or Japanese
banks?) on security of gold in transit but are continuing
to make loans on security of bonds. Mr. Harrison wondered
whether we would wish to make any comment. He thought
that we would be interested in having the information.
I said that I would convey the information to the
Secretary at the earliest possible moment.
F/JA

ADVISER ON
FA/
DIVIBIUN OF
September 28, 1937.
FILE - JAPAN
894.51
Mr. Lochhead telephoned to state that his further
investigation in New York had disclosed that Japanese
894.51/510
banks had net assets in New York of 14 million dollars.
The figure which I obtained last week from him concern-
ing Japanese balances in New York covered only the
Federal Reserve Bank and New York branches of Japanese
banks, and in these banks there were disclosed no bal-
ances but a net million-dollar overdraft. (This latter
figure was used in a recent report on Japan's foreign
F/MR
exchange resources.) The figure of 14 million dollars
is one which Mr. Lochhead considers vory accurate.
Mr. Lochhead also stated that a shipment of gold
valued at 4 million dollars had been shipped directly
from Japan to London more or less as an experiment but
that it was unsuccessful and will probably not be
repeated.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dr Feis:
894.5
The situation presented in the memorandum enclosed
with Mr. Lancaster's letter of October 6, 1937, as I
understand it, is somewhat as follows:
51/5
The National City Bank in New York has opened, and
is now opening, credits on behalf of Oriental banks to
be drawn on in payment for goods exported from the
United States. The credits, so it appears, were and are
2
being established through applications made by merchants
in the Far East to independent banks in China and Japan
or to foreign branches of the National City Bank.
On
establishing the credit the National City Bank agrees to
accept drafts drawn on it to the extent of the credit when
accompanied by the bills of lading and certain other docu-
ments covering the exports. The notification sent by the
Bank in response to the application for the establishment
of credit contains the provision that: "The engagement
F
/WB
and undertaking referred to in this paragraph being binding
only if the shipments proposed shall be permitted under
the

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
the Amerioan Neutrality Act of August 31, 1935, as amended,
and such proclamations, orders and regulations as may be
issued thereunder."
The Bank apparently envisages the possibility of
certain difficulties with respect to these transactions in
the event of a proclamation under the so-called Neutrality
Act of May 1, 1937, and desires that some exception or
exceptions should be made in the proclamation with respect
to these transactions. The Bank refers specifically to
Section 1(a), Section 2(a) and (b), and Section 3(a).
Section 1(a) provides for the placing of an embargo
on the exportation of arms, ammunition, and implements
of war. Shipments of these articles would, therefore,
be unlawful after the issuance of a proclamation and,
consequently, the agreement by the Bank to establish and
maintain a credit would be safeguarded by the above-quoted
provision incorporated in its acceptance and no obligation
to honor drafts covering shipments of this kind would arise.
Section 2(a) merely authorizes the President in his
discretion to issue a proclamation specifying certain
articles which it shall be unlawful for American ships
to carry. If such a proclamation should be issued, the
shipment of the specified articles on American vessels
would be unlawful, and hence the Bank would, under its
above-quoted reservation, be under no obligation to pay the
draft.

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
draft.
The difficulty which the Bank contemplates of
ascertaining whether shipment is to be carried on an
American vessel is one which can not be covered by a
regulation. The regulations authorized by Section 2(a)
have to do only with lakes, rivers, and inland waters
bordering on the United States.
Section 2(b) - the so-called (cash and carry provision -
authorizes the President in his discretion and under
certain circumstances to issue a proolamation, making it
illegal to export any article from the United States to
belligerent states, etc., until all right, title and
interest therein shall have been transferred to a foreign
government, agency, etc.
If by negotiating a draft with the bills of lading
and other shipping documents attached the Bank would
have an interest in the goods, the shipment of such goods
following a proclamation under Section 2(b) would be
illegal. Here again the Bank would seem to be relieved
of its obligation by the above-quoted reservation. The
President has no authority to make exceptions to
Section 2(b) save as to lakes, rivers, and inland waters
bordering on the United States.
Section 3(a) provides that whenever the President
has issued a proclamation under Section 1, namely, that
there exists a state of war or of civil strife, it shall
thereafter

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
thereafter be unlawful for any person in the United
States to purchase, sell, or exchange bonds, securities,
or other obligations of the government of a belligerent
state or of a state wherein civil strife exists, or of
a political subdivision thereof, or of any person acting
for or on behalf of the government of any such state, etc.
This section comes into operation automatically.
Credits
of the character referred to by the National City Bank
might or might not be prohibited, depending upon the
relationship of the beneficiary of the credit to the
belligerent government, that is to say, whether the
beneficiary is acting for or on behalf of that government.
The section contains a proviso under which the
President may, if he finds such act would serve to pro-
tect the commeroial or other interests of the United
States or its citizens, issue regulations excepting
from the operation of the section "ordinary commercial
credits and short-time obligations in aid of legal trans-
actions and of a character customarily used in normal
peacetime commercial transactions."
While the President could, if he saw fit, issue
regulations of the character here indicated, he could
not by such regulations modify or limit the effects of
the prohibitions contained in Section 1, or those
contained in Section 2(a) or (b), if proclamations had
been

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
been issued under these last mentioned provisions.
It is noted that the Bank's reservation in its
notification of the establishment of the credit (Exhibit B)
refers to the Neutrality Act of August 31, 1935, which,
of course, is no longer in existence. I would suppose
that this has been changed since the passage of the Act
of 1937.
Green H. Hackworth.

MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1937
1039
Mr. Hackworth:
NIGATIONS
EA aske for the construction by this office of the per-
F.W.
tinent part of Section 3 of the Neutrality Act having
reference to two hypothetical cases, both relating to
the status of branch banks in foreign countries.
894.51/514
Section 3 of the Neutrality Act of 1937 has not
been passed upon by any federal court. The conclusion
reached, considering the purpose and scope of the
statute, must necessarily be on a broad rather than
technical legal basis. The principle applied in the
reported cases as interpreted by the courts in usual
2
commercial transactions is one of agency. The decisions
are fairly uniform in holding that a national bank and
its foreign branches organized under the National Banking
Act (it is presumed that this Act would be applicable in
the instant case) are separate entities in business trans-
F/WB
MAR 18 1989
actions between them, but when considered with relation
FILED
to the parent bank, they are not independent agencies;
they are, what their name imports, merely branches, and
are subject to the supervision and control of the parent
bank, and are instrumentalities whereby the parent bank
carries

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
carries on its business. Applying this principle to the
two cases cited in the memorandum, it would seem that if
the transactions were carried out completely by the
branches of the National City Bank located in China or
Japan, Section 3 would not apply. I feel, however, that
the Department cannot lay down such a sweeping interpreta-
tion without having the details of each transaction before
it. Under the circumstances, therefore, it would seem
that both questions should be answered in the affirmative.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1937
In Murtaugh v. Yokohama Specie Bank, Limited, 269
N. Y. S., it appeared that Maki, a citizen of Japan,
procured from time to time during 1927 and 1928 thir-
teen drafts for 3,870.68 Japanese yen at the New York
branch of the defendant and forwarded the said drafts
to the main bank or office in Japan to be placed there
as a fixed deposit for one year at 5 per cent. When
the drafts were received in the main office and the de-
posit made to the credit of Maki, a certificate of
deposit was sent back to the New York office and Maki
was notified that this certificate of deposit would be
exchanged for the duplicate draft that was issued to
him when he bought the draft at the branch office. Maki
was duly adjudicated insane by a Pennsylvania court and
subsequently on March 13, 1933, Edward Murtaugh, his
duly appointed committee in Pennsylvania, was appointed
ancillary committee in New York, and as such, brought an
action. In considering the case, the court said:
"[1-3] While many points of law are raised by
the briefs, only one needs to be considered, as the
decision on that point makes it unnecessary to con-
sider the others. It is conceded by the plaintiff
that 'the money was sent to Japan and was on de-
posit there in the head office.' That it was sent
there

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
there by special request of Maki as a fixed de-
posit for one year and thereafter to remain as
a fixed deposit unless other arrangements were
made, is established by the documentary evi-
dence. (Defendant's Exhibits A, B, and D.)
The evidence establishes that the demand for
payment was made on the New York branch and
that no demand was made at the branch where the
money is on deposit. 'Deposits made in a
branch bank are payable there and there only ...
except, if the branch be closed or if demand
for payment by the depositor be refused, then
demand will lie against the parent bank.' Blue-
bird Undergarment Corporation v. Gomez, 139
Misc. 742, 744, 249 N. Y. S. 319, 321, and cases
therein cited.
"The same rule was enunciated in Sokoloff
v. National City Bank of New York, 25O N. Y. 69,
81, 82, 164 N. E. 745, where the parent bank
which was the depository was stated to be the
place where a demand was to be made unless it
was closed or demand refused. Thereafter, be-
cause of the breach of contract, he may recover
his damages in New York where he sought his
remedy in dollar value at the time of demand at
the foreign depository. Plaintiff argues that
because the depositor is insane and cannot make a
demand at Yokohama, the rule should be different.
While there may be hardship under the circum-
stances, it is manifest that the committee has no
rights arising out of the contract of deposit
greater than those that the depositor himself
had. .Upon the evidence the complaint is dis-
missed. Exception to plaintiff, who is allowed
a stay of twenty days and sixty days to make a
case."
In the British case of Richardson v. Richardson,
43 Law Reports, Probate Division, 1927, page 234, it was
held that primarily a bank was liable to pay only at its
branch upon demand; that upon demand and refusal to pay at
its branch, the parent bank could be sued, but that the
nade
breach having ben a refusal to pay in a foreign currency,
the cause of action could only be for damages. The bank
could

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.95)
-3-
could not be sued for money lent.
Le:AO'N:RMB:SS

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1937.
In the Bluebird Undergarment Corporation v. Gomez,
249 N. Y. S., Page 319, attachment proceedings were
instituted by the Bluebird Undergarment Corporation
against the Gomez Embroidery Company, in which an
attachment warrant was served on the National City Bank
of New York. The plaintiff obtained an order requiring
the bank to submit to examination concerning property of
the defendant, and moved to require the bank to serve a
certificate on the sheriff setting out information con-
cerning a deposit of the defendant's in their Porto Rico
branch.
In speaking of the transaction, the court stated:
"The right to this order and the power of the
court to grant it depend upon the determination of
the situs of the debt which was created by the
alleged deposit in the Porto Rico branch. The
question presented is of far-reaching importance to
the commercial and banking worlds. It is claimed
by the plaintiff, and it may be assumed, that the
defendant has moneys on deposit in the bank's branch
in Porto Rico. It thus becomes necessary to estab-
lish the relationship existing between a parent bank
and the depositors of its branches.
"It has been held that a corporation which con-
ducts business, makes contracts, and incurs debts
in another jurisdiction becomes domiciled in that
jurisdiction in respect to suoh business, and debts
incurred there are there attachable. Lancaster v.
Spotswood, 41 Misc. Rep. 19, 83 N. Y. S. 572, af-
firmed

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
firmed 86 App. Div. 627, 83 N. Y. S. 1109; Morgan
v. Mutual Benefit Life Íns. Co., 189 N. Y. 447, 82
N. E. 438; Lancaster Ins. Co. v. Corbetts, 165 Ill.
592, 46 N. E. 631, 36 L. R. A. 640, 56 Am. St. Rep.
275.
"[1] A branch bank is a separate and distinot
business entity. USCA title 12, 8 604; Pan-American
Bank & Trust Co. v. National City Bank of New York
(0. C. A.) 6 F. (2d) 762, 766; Chrzanowska v. Corn
Exchange Bank, 173 App. Div. 285, 159 N. Y. S. 385,
affirmed 225 N. Y. 728, 122 N. E. 877. In the Pan-
American Case, supra, Hough, C. J., writing for
the court, said:
"•... The Rio branch of City Bank existed
under the Act of December 23, 1913, and its
amendments (U. S. Comp. Stat. 8 9745). That
statute requires inter alia, that national
banks having foreign branches "shall conduct
the accounts of each foreign branch independ-
ently of the accounts ... of its home office,
and shall at the end of each fiscal period
transfer to the general ledger the profit or
loss accrued at each branch as a separate
item." That is, the branch is not a mere
"teller's window"; it is a separate business
entity.'
"Likewise in Chrzanowska v. Corn Exchange
Bank, supra, page 291 of 173 App. Div. 159 N. Y. S.
385, 388, the court said:
""... With respect to the question presented
for decision, the different branches were as
separate and distinct from one another as from
any other bank. The Legislature did not in-
tend, we think, either to authorize or require
a bank having branches to cash checks and make
loans to a depositor at any branch at which he
may see fit to call, for to do so would produce
endless confusion, and the statutory provisions
quoted do not require such a construction.'
"[2, 3] Not only are branch banks separate
entities, but deposits made in a branch bank are
payable there and there only (Chrzanowska v. Corn
Exchange Bank, supra; Pan-American Bank & Trust Co.
v. National City Bank of New York, supra; Sokoloff
v.

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
v. National City Bank of New York, 130 Misc. Rep.
66, 224 N, Y. S. 102, affirmed 250 N. Y. 69, 164
N. E. 745); except, if the branch be closed or
if demand for payment by the depositor be refused,
then demand wiil lie against the parent bank
(Sokoloff v. National City Bank of New York, 250
N. Y. 69, 164 N. E. 745). In the instant case the
branch in Porto Rico is not closed, nor does it
appear that such branch has refused payment. The
defendant could not commence an action against the
bank in New York for the deposit made by him in
the Porto Rico branch. A branch bank being
separately indebted to its depositor, the existing
obligation lies primarily between such branch bank
and its depositor. The conclusion follows as a
necessary corollary that the debt owed by a branch
finds its situs within the territorial jurisdiction
of such branch. Although the branch deposit be the
ultimate debt of the parent bank, the obligation to
its customer, however, is localized so as to be con-
fined primarily to the branch where it originated."
"My conclusions are that the situs of the debt
is in Porto Rico, that the bank is not indebted to
the defendant in New York county, and therefore has
no property within said county belonging to the de-
fendant, and that attachment in New York county may
not be availed of. Proceedings predicated thereon
must necessarily be limited to property within
this jurisdiction subject to attachment, and cannot
be extended to property situated elsewhere.' Stine
v. Greene, 65 App. Div. 221, 223, 72 N. Y. S. 729,
731.
"The motion is accordingly denied. Submit
order on notice."
Le:AO'N:RMB:SS

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1937.
In Chrzanowska v. Corn Exchange Bank, 159 N. Y. S.,
status
page 385, the question of the statutes of a branch bank
situated in the same city was before the court, and the
language used in defining its relationship to the parent
bank was as follows:
"The defendant required its depositors to agree
to draw their checks on the particular branch in
which they were depositors, and the checks were so
drawn and the accounts were so kept. With respect
to the question presented for decision, the differ-
ent branches were as separate and distinct from one
another as from any other bank. The Legislature
did not intend, we think, either to authorize or
require a bank having branches to cash checks and
make loans to a depositor at any branch at which he
may see fit to call, for to do so would produce end-
less confusion, and the statutory provisions quoted
do not require such a construction. If the check
had been drawn on another bank, there could be no
doubt but that such other bank not only might, but
it would have been its duty to, refrain from paying
the check if it had notice of the death of the
drawer. Glennan v. Rochester Trust & Safe Deposit Co.,
supra. If the check had been drawn upon and returned
by another bank, the rule is well settled that the
defendant would have had the right to charge it up
against the depositor's account."
To the same effect was the decision in the Pan-American
Bank and Trust Company v. the National City Bank, 6 Fed.
(2d Series) page 763. Discussing the branch bank, the
court used this language:
"But the Rio branch, in so becoming a holder
of the drafts, and then sending them to New York
for

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
for collection, assumed the same relation to its
home office that any other bank or person that had
similarly negotiated the paper would have assumed.
In fact, as the evidence shows, the drafts and
accompanying documents went from the Rio branch
to the New York home office with a demand for
credit; i.e., a demand for payment, and payment by
the demanded credit was given the branch. This was
just what the cited act of Congress in effect re-
quires, and illustrates the rulings that, in respect
of the collection of forwarded paper, branches and a
parent bank are to be considered as separate enti-
ties. Woodland v. Fear, 7 E. & B. 5l9; Clode v.
Baylay, 12 M. & W. 579; McNeil v. Wyatt, 3 Humph.
(Tenn.) 125, considering the point generally; U. S.
Bank v. Goddard, 5 Mason, 366, Fed. Cas. No. 917,
under the statutes regulating the Bank of the United
States; and Chrzanowska v. Corn Exchange Bank, 173
App. Div. 285, 159 N. Y. S. 385, affirmed 225 N. Y.
728, 122 N. E. 877, under statutes regulating banks
chartered by the state."
Le:AO'N:RMB:SS

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1937.
In Sokoloff v. National City Bank, 224 N. Y. S.,
pages 102-129, the facts are these:
The plaintiff deposited with the National City
Bank of New York money for the purpose of opening an
account in its Russian branch, and received therefor
a receipt that the money paid represented cost of a
certain number of rubles to be transferred to the
Russian bank to open plaintiff's account; there was
only one contract made with the bank of New York, to
be carried out in part by its Russian branch, and in
a separate contract with the Russian branch.
In considering the case, the court stated:
"[4] Where a bank maintains branches, each
branch becomes a separate business entity, with
separate books of account. A depositor in one
branch cannot issue checks or drafts upon another
branch or demand payment from such other branch,
and in many other respects the branches are con-
sidered separate corporate entities and as
distinct from one another as any other bank.
Chrzanowska v. Corn Exchange Bank, 173 App. Div.
285, 159 N. Y. S. 385; Pan-American Bank & Trust
Co. v. National City Bank of New York (C. C. A.)
6 F. (2d) 762, 767; Woodland v. Fear, 7 E1. & B1.
519; U. S. Comp. Stat.  9745 (12 US0A 8S 601-605);
Federal Reserve Act, 8 25 (Act Dec. 23, 1913, c. 6,
38 Stat. 273. Nevertheless, when considered with
relation to the parent bank, they are not
independent agencies; they are, what their name
imports, merely branches, and are subject to the
supervision

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
supervision and control of the parent bank, and
are instrumentalities whereby the parent bank
carries on its business, and are established for
its own particular purposes, and their business
conduct and policies are controlled by the parent
bank, and their property and assets belong to the
parent bank, although nominally held in the names
of the particular branches. United States Bank v.
Goddard, 5 Mason, 366, Fed. Cas. No. 917. Ultimate
liability for a debt of a branch would rest upon
the parent bank."
In the instant case the court held the contract was
made in New York; that the defendant had undertaken to
pay, on demand, in Russia, in rubles; it wholly failed to
do so, because it had removed its branch from Petrograd;
that the only form open to the plaintiff in which to
assert his rights was the New York courts; and that the
plaintiff was entitled to receive the ruble equivalent
in dollars at the time it should have been paid.

CREDIT AND THE NEUTRALITY ACT
MAR13 1939
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
F.W.894.51/514
The National City Bank in a recent memorandum
points out that certain commitments which it is making
daily in the regular course of its international busi-
ness may be in conflict with the neutrality law, should
proclamations be issued by the President pursuant to
Sections 1, 2(a) and 2(b) regarding exports to China
and Japan. The Bank asserts that embarrassments to
itself are likely to result if proclamations become
1
effective on the date of issuance or without certain
2
reservations, foreclosing to the Bank the opportunity
of adjusting its outstanding commitments or of being
otherwise protected against civil or criminal liabili-
ties resulting from new legal restrictions following
from the proclamations.
The questions raised by the National City Bank are
F/WB
FTLED
almost exclusively of a legal character. The remarks
presented in this memorandum are intended only to out-
line the questions at issue.
The

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
12
The Bank's inquiries relate entirely to its commit-
ments under irrevocable (and presumably also, confirmed)
export letters of credit. Subject to the conditions
noted in the written contracts, the Bank is irrevocably
bound to negotiate drafts drawn by the exporter on the
Bank under credits issued in behalf of the oriental
importer.or the importer's bank.
The Bank's memorandum points out that in notifica-
tions to beneficiaries under credit letters the follow-
ing clause is now included:
"The engagement and undertaking referred to
in this paragraph being binding only if the ship-
ment(s) proposed shall be permitted under the
American Neutrality Act of August 31, 1935, as
amended, and such proclamations, orders, and
regulations as may be issued thereunder."
Apparently counsel for the National City Bank is not
entirely confident that the Bank is fully covered by
this clause as to its liabilities. Whether this is so
is not within the competence of this discussion to judge.
At least one important situation which the Bank has
in mind, indicated on page 4, is the case in which credit
terms are arranged covering goods which have to be
specially manufactured, and hence have a very limited
general market. Faced with the impossibility of getting
his goods, the oriental merchant may naturally be expected
to resist attempts to hold him to his contract. The
manufacturer, having been granted an irrevocable privilege
of

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
of drawing on the Bank, may be expected to resist any
obtain reimbursement from the Epposter.
effort the Bank might make to redeem its promise.
Hence, the prospect is that the Bank will find itself
owning an inventory of goods having a questionable
market. That the saving clause just quoted should not
establish the contractual right of the Bank to redeem
its promise seems strange. But the Bank seems to have
doubts on the matter, so we must go on.
Two methods of meeting the problems are suggested
in the National City Bank's memorandum:
(1) To make the effective dates of proclamations
later than the dates of issuance.
(2) For proclamations, if and when issued, to
contain such reservations and otherwise be made so
explicit that the rights of the parties may be clear.
As for the first alternative, the phrasing of
Sections 1, 2(a) and 2(b) of the neutrality law do not
appear to provide the flexibility necessary for delay-
gagee
ing the effective date of proclamations beyond the date
of issuance. The basis of this judgment is in the fol-
lowing passages from the law:
In Section 1(a) and (c) there appears the
specification that after a finding by the President
as to the existence of a state of war or civil
strife, ".
. . the President shall proclaim such
fact, and it shall thereafter be unlawful . . .
Sections 2(a)

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
Sections 2(a) and 2(b) similarly specify
that after certain additional findings by the
President, "
. he shall so proclaim, and it
shall thereafter be unlawful .
(The 1im-
ited exception stated in the section cannot
refer to the case in point.)
Congressional intention in these phrases seems
clearly to be that dates of issuance and effectiveness
of proclamations shall be identical.
As a second alternative method of meeting its prob
lem the Bank suggests: "That if, and when, proclamations
are issued, they may contain such reservations and other
wise be made so explicit that the rights of the parties
may be clear and the necessity for any such form of pro-
tection (i.e., by means of the saving clause) thereafter
thus be obviated." The inclusion in an executive procla-
mation of such detailed reservations, defining the rights
of parties in interest, is unlikely to be acceded to by
this Government. The intricate points of contract law
which such a proclamation would have to penetrate seem
to warn against such inclusion. The Government, in all
its astuteness, probably could not foresee or fore-weigh
the various conflicts of rights which particular cases
will involve. By following the Bank'g second suggestion,
the Government would probably find itself eventually
having taken sides with one or another of the parties in
interest.
Consequently

=== Page 341 === (confidence 1.00)
5
Consequently neither suggestion put forth in the
Bank's memorandum seems tenable. There is, further,
the technical question of law (centering on the saving
clause) as to whether the Bank is not already adequately
protected in possible civil or criminal litigation. It
1s scarcely conceivable that this Government or the
courts are going to be so unreasonable as to overlook
acts in good faith when the Bank can prove it has so
acted; and the Bank undoubtedly is taking every pre-
caution in this direction.

=== Page 342 === (confidence 1.00)
6
Some Further Notes
The following are brief notations of and comments
on some of the questions raised in the Bank's memorandum.
1. Part of the Bank's concern, as expressed in its
memorandum, arises from the effect the Neutrality Act
may be having on the Bank's competitive position in
getting export financing business. The saving clause
eliminates a guaranty which American exporters do not
like to relinquish, and they are therefore under induce-
ment to finance through foreign banks which are not so
restricted.
2. The civil litigation of which the Bank is
apprehensive would arise generally from three situa-
tions:
a) At the time the specific shipments are
declared illegal the Bank will already have negotiated
drafts drawn by the exporter. In this case the Bank
may find itself the holder of merchandise collateral
which cannot be liquidated (the oriental importer refus-
ing to pay for merchandise which cannot be shipped)
except at a loss. The legal question is that of the
Bank's recourse to the exporter. The Bank's memorandum
suggests that the confirming bank has no recourse to
the exporter.
b) Before

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
一7
b) Before the confirming bank has negotiated
the exporter's drafts, but the credit letter contract
having already been completed, the shipment becomes
illegal. The Bank has an irrevocable commitment to
negotiate the exporter's drafts on an illegal shipment.
c) The exporter is a resident alien or for-
2(6)
eign corporation. Under Section & of the Neutrality
Act such an exporter's oath that no American interest
exists in a particular bill of goods for export would
presumably be valid. Having received confirmation of
the Bank's agreement to negotiate his drafts, the ex-
porter presents the draft and lading documents. If the
Bank negotiates the drafts it is said that the effect
is "to inject an American interest in the goods prob-
ably sufficient to prevent their export, unless appro-
priate exceptions to cover routine commercial transac-
tions other than export should be made in the proclama-
tion. (Note Section 3(a).) If it refuses it may be
sued upon its express contract to negotiate, and, if it
does negotiate the draft the goods become unexportable."
(Page 3) In the latter event the Bank is in a position
similar to that noted in paragraph (a) above.
3. Regarding the statement in paragraph 2 (c) above,
". . . unless appropriate exceptions to cover routine
commercial

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
commercial transactions other than export should be
made in the proclamation (note Section 3 (a))," the
relevance of Section 3 (a) to the situation is doubt-
ful. The phrase in Section 3(a) which is in point
reads as follows:
"Provided, that if the President shall find
that such action will serve to protect the commer-
cial or other interests of the United States or
its citizens, he may, in his discretion, and to
such extent and under such regulations as he may
prescribe, except from the operation of this sec-
tion of the commercial credits -and short-time
obligations in aid of legal transactions and of a
character customarily used in normal peacetime
commercial transactions."
Is the latitude given the executive in this proviso of
such character as to allow qualification of a proclama-
tion under Section 2(b), which contains no explicit
provision for an exception touching on the case in
point?
4. The Bank's memorandum deals with three classes
of cases, which are anticipated with respect to Sections
1, 2(a) and 2(b), respectively, of the Neutrality Act.
A proclamation under Section 2(a) would prohibit
exportation of certain named articles in American ships.
The Bank anticipates that "no difficulty would be
experienced in these cases if time were given after the
issuance of a proclamation in which to reroute the con-
signment for shipment under a foreign flag unless the
goods

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
9
goods were inadvertently misdirected in the invoice."
No indication is given in the memorandum as to how much
time would be needed to accomplish this rerouting. Fur
ther, as indicated above, it seems unlikely that such a
time allowance can legally be made.
5. The Bank points to the "railroad through bill
of lading" as one source of potential difficulty. Ocean
bills of lading are not regularly required in letter of
credit transactions covering exportations by Pacific
ports to the Far East. In the case of railroad through
bills of lading "weeks may elapse between the time the
consignment is placed on freight cars at the interior
shipping point and the time when it reaches a seaport
and the exporting ship crosses the three mile limit
outward bound." It further points out that even in the
case of credits for which "on board" or ocean bills of
lading are required, there is always a chance that com-
plications may ensue from the fact that a proclamation
prohibiting export may be issued while the goods are in
the course of lading or before the ship has actually
left the harbor. These facts raise the question as to
whether the time allowance could be great enough to
cover cases which involve "weeks", even should it be
legally possible to postpone in some degree the effec-
tiveness of proclamations.
6.It

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.96)

". . . represents an effort on the part of

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.98)
September 21, 1937. 10.15
rendum to Mr. W.W.Lancaster.
2.15
Re: Neutrality
The Bank feels apprehensive over the possibility that as a result of the Sino-
Japanose situation a proclamation or proclamations may be issued under the Neutrality
Act which may be made effective as of the date of issue leaving no time for adjustment
of outstanding matters prior to such effective dates.
It will be noted that under the Neutrality law three different classes of
proolamations covering exports from this country are possible. The ank is concerned
because it has opened and now has outstanding credits on behalf of Oriental banks
covering exports from America which may be effected by any proclanation which may be
issued under authority of the Neutrality Act and is daily opening new ones of like
character. These credits are opened by merchants in the Orient who apply in the first
instance to a bank in say China or Japan, either an independent institution or one of
the branches of The National City Bank of New York. In either event the prospective
importing merchant is known or is believed by the Bank to be of such credit standing
as to insure the ultimate payment by him of the value of the proposed shipment. Assured
as to his credit responsibility the bank in the Orient advises the National City Bank
of Mew York on a form similar to that attached,marked Exhibit "A", the gist of which
is a request to The National City Bank of New York to negotiate drafts drawn upon it
or upon the Oriental merchant, when accompanied by invoices describing the goods,
imsurance policies of such a type as may be specified (war risk clause,etc.) and bills
of lading covering the goods.
Upom reeeipt of such request the Bank in turn notifies the beneficiary on a form
similar to that attached hereto, marked Exhibit "B",(the clause in red upon this form
ibe later addition and will be referred to below.)

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
omo. to Mr.W.W.Lencaster.
P.2.
The goods in question may be either standard commodities having a world
market such as, cotton, scrap iron, wheat, etc., or they may be specially
manufactured goods. In the latter event the American manufacturer will not ordinarily
undertake the production of such goods involving perhaps new dies and machinery without
assurance that he can be sure of his price for them as soon as the goods are shipped.
This assurance is given to him by the notification to the beneficiary on Exhibit "B"
above referred to. Under the established practice goods for the Orient are not
required, unless the notification so states, to be covered by ocean bills of lading.
Through bills of lading are acceptable and this is particularly true of notifications
heretofore made under credits already opened. Even in the case of credits for which
"on board" or ocean bills of lading are required, there is always a chance that
complications may ensue from the fact that a proclamation baring export may be issued
while the goods are in course of lading or before the ship has actually left the harbor.
This danger is vastly increased in the case of through bills of lading, since weeks
may elapse between the time the consignment is placed on freight cars at the interior
shipping point and the time when it reaches a seaport and the exporting ship crosses
the three mile limit outward bound.
As suggested above the anticipated proclamation may fall into any one of
three categories.
First, a simple finding that a state of war exists under Section I-A of the
law. No especial anxiety is felt in regard to this class of cases since the exporter
is by hypothesis already acting under license and cannot hope to obtain a bill of lading
without presentation of a proper exporter's oath. There is still some little chance of
difficulty in the case of through bills of lading since the exporter's oath may have

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
to Mr. W.W.Lancaster.
P.3.
been taken at the interior port and conditions may be entirely changed before the
goods reach the seaport.
The second class of proclamation (under Section 2-A) would prohibit
export of certain named articles in American bottoms. No difficulty would be
experienced in these cases if time were given after the issuance of the proclamation
in which to re-route the consignment for shipment under a foreign flag unless the
goods were inadvertently misdescribed in the invoice.
The third class of proclamation (under Section 2-B) would cover what is
popularly known as the "cash and carry" provisions. Such a proclamation would
prohibit the export from America to any belligerent country of any merchandise in which
an American citizen had any interest. Under such a proclamation various difficulties
are sure to arise. The exporter is obliged to take oath that no American interest
exists and if he happens to be a resident alien or a foreign corporation doing business
in this country he will have no difficulty in taking such an oath so that the papers
apparently will all be in order. If thereafter, however, he brings the documents to
an American bank already committed by contract and  under legal obligation to
negotiate the draft thereby accepting the bill of lading as its security for the
final payment of the amount advanced to the exporter, the effect of such negotiation
is to inject an American interest in the goods probably sufficient to prevent their
export, unless appropriate exceptions to cover routine commercial transactions other
than export should be made in the proclamation. (Note Section 3-a) Without the red
clause above referred to the Bank would be in the position where it must either
negotiate or refuse to negotiate. If it refuses it may be sued upon its express
contract to negotiate, and, on the other hand, if it does negotiate the draft the goods
becomeunexportable.

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
omo. to Mr. W.w.Lancaster.
P.4.
There will always be a hope, of course, that the Oriental buyer will honor
his engagements and pay for his goods even when he knows that they are indefinitely
detained in this country, but this is by no means certain, and faced with the
impossibility of getting his goods the Oriental merchant may naturally be expected to
resist attempts to hold him to his contract to the best of his ability. The courts
of his own country would doubtless sympathize with him in his efforts. In such a
case should the goods be specially manufactured, and hence have a very limited general
market, the Bank is quite apt to suffer a substantial loss and this is particularly
true in cases where the credit has been opened at the instance of one of its own
branches. If a time were to be granted between the issuance of a "cash and carry"
proclamation and its effective date the Bank might be able to avoid this class of
difficulties by arranging for the negotiating of the draft covering the goods through
some foreign bank.
In the foregoing mention has been made of the civil phases of the subject
only. There is also a criminal aspect. The Bank becomes bound by its notification to
the beneficiary (Exhibit "B" above referred to) and this contract at the time it is
made is perfectly legal and binding. If after the effective date of a proclamation
an attempt is made to export the goods the officers of the Bank might be accused of
being parties to an illegal exportation. In other words as being guilty of a crime.
Note the provision that an exporter's oath estops all American interest including
apparently that of a bank ignorant of the material facts. As one illustration of the
way in which difficulties might arise under a proclamation forbidding exports in
American vessels, suppose that the invoice accompanying a draft contains terms which

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr.W.W.Lancaster.
P.5.
are ambiguous the goods actually being merchandise on the prohibited list which cannot
lawfully be exported in an American vessel. The Bank, however, deceived by the terms
of the invoice negotiates the drafts and forwards the bill of lading to its Oriental
correspondent. The charge is subsequently made that a more careful reading of the
invoice should have convinced the Bank that the goods were prohibited exports and that
it ought not to be negotiated.
The red clause upon the notification (Exhibit "B") represents an effort on
the part of the Bank to do two things.
First,to warn prospective exporters that the goods covered by their documents
may become unexportable in which event the Bank will decline responsibility. In other
words it is a warning that the American exporter must take the risk under any
proclamation which may be issued.
Second, the Bank gives notice that it will not knowingly be a party to any
illegal transaction and the clause is hence an effort to avoid being put in a position
where the criminal law might be invoked against it or its officers.
The necessity for the use of this red clause is regretted since its incidental
effect is to drive exporters, particularly aliens or foreign corporations doing business
here, to deal with the English banks rather than the American, and it also tends to
drive business to such American banks as are willing to negotiate documents without
demanding such protection. It is hoped that if, and when, proclamations are issued they
may contain such reservations and otherwise be made so explicit that the rights of the
parties may be clear and the necessity for any such form of protection thereafter thus
be obviated.
Wartred Beaumnt
HB:og.
Hartford Beaumont.

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.98)
CREDIT NO.

GOVERNMENT FINANCE:
After having approved supplementary budgets of ¥10,000,000
and ¥96,800,000 in July for financing Japanese military opera-
tions in China, in the first week of August the Diet approved
for the same purpose an additional supplementery budget of
¥419,635,200.*
The allotments of the last named supplementary
budget, which was promulg&ted on August 9, are roughly es fol-
lows: ¥257,000,000 to the War Ministry; ¥95,000,000 to the Navy
Ministry; ¥63,500,000 to the Finance Ministry; and ¥4,000,000
to the Foreign Office.**
On August 2 the Cabinet approved provisional increase for
one year of the income tax and severel other taxes, the increese
to yield an additional revenue of ¥102,000,000 to be applied
to the expenses of militery operations in North China.***
The
tax increase wes subsequently approved by the Dict. Apropos
of the tax increase, Mr. Toyotaro Yuki, Governor of the Bank
of Japan, stated to press representetives on August 2 that the
Government authorities were careful not to place the burden
of taxation on the masses in the present tax increese; that
the North China incident was but temporary in nature and cor-
respondingly the present measure of tex increase was but tem-
porary; end that the part of the expenses of the North China
Incident which could not be covered by this new source of revenue
would be met by public loans.
It was quite apparent, even during thc early part of the
month, that if the China situation developed into an extensive
campaign Japan would be forced to import considerably more raw
meterials for munitions, which would necessitate the further
shipment

Professor James T. Shotwell called me on the
telephone from New York and raised as a matter of
speculation the question whether something might not
be done along the line of refusing to buy gold from
894.51/517
Japan. I replied that it must be kept in mind that
popular opinion has been expressed in this country
to the effect that this country must "keep out of war"
and toward that end must pursue a policy of neutrality.
I said that I was not possessed of sufficient knowledge
of problems of exchange to feel warranted even in try-
ing to have an opinion as to what might be done in
T
connection with gold or of what effects might possibly
MR
be achieved in that connection; and I suggested that,
if he felt inclined to study that question, Professor
Shotwell might confer with some of his friends in New
York who know about or are supposed to know about such
matters.
Professor Shotwell expressed great concern over
the present situation in the Far East. He inquired
whether I had seen Dr. Nicholas Murray Butler's statement.
I said that I had seen a newspaper account of it.
The

Professor Shotwell's part of sympathy with and best
wishes for officers of the Department who are struggling
with problems such as those to which he had been
referring.

1

501
subject. The Embassy's despatch No, 2513, July 24, 1937
transmitted the basic budget of the fiscal year April 1,
1937 to March 31, 1938 as promulgated on March 30, 1937;
the present despatch aims at summarizing, up to the present

=== Page 366 === (confidence 1.00)

The basic 1937-l938 general budget, it may be recalled,
authorized general budget expenditures of ¥2,813,937,971.
This basic general budget of ¥2,813,937,971 in itself was
huge by Japanese standards. The corresponding figure for
the preceding fiscal year had been ¥2,307,000,000, and in
1931-1932 had been ¥1,476,000,000.
That basic budget was approved by the 7Oth Diet, meeting
in March, and was promulgated on March 30, 1937. Since then
there have been two other sessions of the Diet, the 7lst,
meeting from late July to early August, and the 72nd, meeting
in early September. Both approved modifications of the bud-
get which greatly augment the total. The present general
account total authorized expenditures can be set forth as

With regard to disclosure of information concerning the bud-
get the country is already on a full war basis. But, from
several indications, it can be taken as fairly certain that
the expectation is that this two billion yen will provide
for the period pending early January 1938 when the regular
Diet session gets down to work. Two billion yen is the sum
authorized for the direct money costs of four months of
hostilities in China--another proof of the magnitude of
Japan's view of the campaign, no matter how undeclared this
war may be.
It can be stated with confidence that the ¥5,474,566,294
thus far appropriated will not meet the demands of the general
account through the fiscal year (ending March 31).
Only some
completely unforeseen turn of events in China could intervene
to make unnecessary additional appropriations by the next
Diet.
The demand for funds seems far from exhausted. The
general account budget already exceeds its level of last
year by three billion yen and the end is not in sight.
What recourse to borrowing has been caused by these
huge appropriations?
The budget promulgated March 30, 1937 carried provision
for public 1oans in the sum of ¥770,823,677 as part of the
revenue to cover the ¥2,813,937,971 appropriated in the
general account. The supplementary budget of ¥58,197,902
promulgated the same day carried provision for public loans
in the sum of ¥51,627,902 toward covering such supplementary
budget.

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
budget. Law No. 49, July 29, 1937,was authority for the
issue of public bonds to the sum of ¥96,000,000 for the North
China incident.
Law No. 50, August 9, 1937, amended this sum
to ¥406,200,000. Law No. 84, September 9, 1937, authorized
borrowings by the Government for the China incident to the
sum of ¥2,022,700,000. Both Law No. 84, and Law No. 49 as
amended by Law No. 50, contain the safeguarding clause that
if bonds which may be issued fail to realize their face
value, that is if the market will not take them at par, addi-
tional bonds may be issued to yield the difference. This is
an interesting provision in anticipation of marketing diffi-
culties.
In tabular form this authorized borrowing by the
Government is as follows:
March 30, 1937. General budget 1oan ¥ 770,823,677
March 30, 1937. Supplementary
general budget loan
51,627,902
August 9, 1937. North China incident
loan
406,200,000
September 9,1937 China incident 1oan 2,022,700,000
¥3,251,351,579
The Japanese Government is borrowing, on account of the fight-
ing in China, a sum in excess of the entire general account
budget of last year; and more seems to be ahead.
The total of public loans for financing of the general
account budget should not be considered the sole measure of
the Government's need of credit. The Japanese budget system
is a system which groups general governmental needs under
one general account, and (entirely separate from the general
account) sets up any number of special accounts for separate
projects. Many of these special accounts have required and
will require financial aid from the Government with funds
raised

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.92)
-5-
raised by the Government through borrowing. Such additional
credit requirements are not under discussion in the present
despatch.

Mr. Lancaster of the National City Bank of New York
called, accompanied by Mr. Rentschler, president of the

793.94112
Mr. Rentschler stated that they had called to tell me

ence to the present situation in the Far East. He said

that the commercial credit transactions handled by the

exports to Japan. He said that the exports consisted

He said that the item of machinery and tools amounted in

that this item undoubtedly was used in Japan largely in
connection with the munitions industry. He said that
since the fighting broke out in China in July the National
City Bank had been pursuing a quiet course but had been
handling such business as came to it. He said that not-
withstanding any loss that might accrue to the National
City Bank as a result of any action which the bank might

adopt, he wished to assure us that the National City Bank

epartment of State
Dear Sir:
I have before me the letter which I received from your
office on this day (see FE 994.51/522).
I an very much interested in the statement therein to
the effect that I am "in error in supposing that there is
any requirement that loans of American banks to any foreign
borrower be approved by the Department of State."
894.51/523
The following quotation is taken from a widely used
text dealing with the foreign policies of the United States:
"In the case of private loans to foreign governments
or corporations floated in the United States, the State
Department since 1922 has adopted the policy of indicating
to the underwriting bankers whether or not such loans are
objectionable from the standpoint of any national interest
involved, without, of course, passing upon the merits of
the loans fron a business standpoint."
I know that such a policy has been followed by the
Department of State in the past and I know that it has been
effective in preventing the floating of particular loans in
the United States. It is that policy to which I referred in
my letter of October 26, 1937.
Am I to understand from your letter of Hovember 5, 1937,
that this policy has been discontinued?
NOV 1 6 1937
PILED
Very truly yours,
D.P Carrall

GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
As already stated, an emergency military budget of over two
billion yen was approved by the Diet on September 9. According
to reliable information received by the Embassy, this emergency
budget was divided into the following items when presented to
the Diet: Army - ¥ 1,422,712,777; Navy - ¥ 349,958,381; and
Reserve - ¥ 250,000,000.
The total budget approved by the end
of September amounted to ¥ 5,474,566,294, a tremendous increase
over the budget for the fiscal year 1931-32 of ¥ 1,476,000,000,
over the budget for 1936-37 of ¥ 2,307,000,000 and also over the
basic budget for 1937-38 approved at the 70th session of the Diet
in March of ￥ 2,813,937,971.***

894.51/52
loans from United States houses to Japan for the
business development of Manchuria and other parts
of the continent. The Ford Motor Company denies the
charge but officials of General Motors refuse to
comment. I hope that you will use the influence of
your great office to check any such loans. I would
even go one step further and suggest thatUnited
States Government refuse to permit any credits to
2
the people and government of Japan during this period
9
of aggression. This would seem to be inherent in the
treaty signed by this country at the Washington Con-
ference of 1921.
For a decade or more after 1919,
due largely to the idealism and untiring exertions of
President Wilson and later to such statesmen as Secre-
tary Hughes, there was a serious attempt throughout
the world to construct a new world order. The Leagu
of Nations, the Nine Power Treaty, the Locarno Pt
and other agreements flowed from these efforts. he
military group in Japan broke this order in Septembe
123l with calamitous results. The objectives of thi6
junta must somehow be checked. The cutting off of
financial aid would seem to be the simplest way.

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Government financial loaders publicly zexpressed optimism
concerning Japan's financial condition. On October 1 Mr. Masa-
take Ota, Parliamentary Vice Minister of Finance, ddclared in
an address before one hundred loading businessmen in Osaka that
the resources of the Japanese nation today would permit of an
expenditure of as much as Yen 40,000,000,000 for military pur-
poses. On October 20 Mr. Toyotaro Yuki, Governor of the Bank
of Japan, stated to newspaper representatives that there was
nothing to worry about concerning the monetary situation in Japan.
Mr. Kaya, the Financo Minister, stated in a press intorview on
October 22 that the Dict would n ot be asked for additional war
funds at its next rogular session; that in his opinion there was
no need for the Government to adopt a general price-fixing policy;
and that the absorption of public bonds was progressing smoothly.
On October 19 the Cabinet approved the Finance Minister's
plan for reducing Governnent expenditures undor the budget for
the current fiscal year by Yen 181,307,000. According to
Domei, the saving is to be accomplished by eliminating some
itoms of public works from the budget and by postponing expen-
ditures under other itoms.
VI.
GENER/L ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a) Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan for October amounted to Yen 297,299,000
and
*
Embassy's telegrom No.484, Octobor 21, and Embassy's
despatch No.2643, October 29, 1937.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
-23-
and imports into Japan amounted to ¥244,406,000, as compared
with exports for September of ¥292,300,000 and imports of
¥263,081,000. This reprosonted a favorable balance of trado of
52,893,000, as compared with a favorable balance for September
of ¥29,219,000.
The declino in imports can be explained partly by the Govern-
ment's rostrictive measuros against imports, and partly by season-
al decline in imports of raw matorials.
The Japanese Government's restrictions on exports deemed
absolutely necessary to the country in the present emergency
period and on imports went into effect on October 10, in accord-
ance with the terms of the law promulgated on September 10.
It was reportod by the press that the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry, after reaching an understanding with Japanese
cotton textile manufacturers and raw cotton dealers, had decided
upon the following plan for the control of the cotton textile
industry: restriction of the importation of raw cotton to
1,500,000 piculs per month; imposition of an import control fee
of ten per cent ad valorem; and the announcement of an official
maximum price of cotton yarn every Saturday. It was estimated
that the restriction of the importation of rew cotton for the
year 1938 would entail a decrease in raw cotton imports of
approximately fifteen per cent as compared with the current
year.

894.51/536
The net conclusions that appear are
(1) Japan would be able to carry on the type of
struggle it is now in with China almost indefinitely,
although at the expense of gradually using up all ex-
9
ternal reservee and therefore indefinitely having to
face all its economic problems without reserves.
(2) However, Japan's resources are limited enough
so that any extended definite conflict with a more
powerful enemy than China would speedily exhaust them
within a year.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
January 4, 1938.
OBSERVATIONS ON JAPAN'S FINANCIAL POSITION
(The present value of the yen is 28.29 cts.)
Mr. Feis:
The article by George E. Anderson in the Washington
894.51
Post of January 2, under the headline "Finance Urges
Japan to Make Peace", represents a more critical situa-
tion in Japanese finances than appears to be warranted
894.51/
by the findings of recent studies made by this office
and by the Treasury Department. The following brief
statement suggests some of the more important qualifi-
cations and extenuations to which Mr. Anderson's con-
5
5
clusions are subject.
9
I. Internal Financing.
Although the appropriations authorised by the Diet
up to September 9 contemplated the raising of approxi-
mately  3300 million by long-term bond issues, actual
emissions up to November 15 amounted to only ¥ 700 mil-
F/MR
lion, with another ¥ 700 million offered in December.
These limited issues may be accounted for, partly by the
large stocks of war materials previously accumulated, partly
by

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
2
by the application of ¥ 332 million of the Treasury's
profit arising from devaluation of the yen in August, to
reduction of its indebtedness to and augmentation of its
deposits with the Bank of Japan, and partly by economies
effected in the General and Special Accounts (to the
reported amount of  l8l million for the current fiscal
year).
Although extensive new bond issues will undoubtedly
have to be emitted during 1938, and although there 1s a
limited body of private investors in Japan, it does not
appear that serious difficulty will be experienced in
having these issues absorbed by the Bank of Japan, the
Deposit Bureau of the Finance Department, the commercial
and savings banks and insurance institutions, with the
Government's recently established exchange stabilisation
fund as an additional element. Moreover, excess profits
of war industries may either be recaptured by the Govern-
ment or be required to be invested in Government securi-
ties. Government bond holdings of the Bank of Japan at
the end of October amounted to ¥ 664 million, compared
to ¥ 816 million preceding devaluation in August and
 454 million at the end of October 1936. Those held by
commercial banks at the end of September, amounting to
* 2,476 million, were ¥ 164 million less than in the
preceding

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
preceding September, and were equivalent to only 19
percent of the earning assets of such banks. The Japan-
ese banking system is in fact less loaded with Govern
ment securities than is the Federal Reserve System, and
has by no means reached its limit of absorptive capacity,
especially if the financing of industries less essential
in the war economy is curtailed. Even though sales of
new Government securities on a large scale are bound to
have an inflationary effect, this can be sustained to a
considerable extent before Japan will be seriously
embarrassed in its internal financing.
II. External Financing.
Although Japan's position is much more vulnerable
on the side of external financing, the situation here
has not yet become really critical. Based on a detailed
analysis of Japan's actual and potential foreign exchange
resources and of the extent to which these are being
used up, the Treasury Department concludes that: "Japan
can continue military operations on the present scale
for at least five months before using up her 'ready!
foreign exohange resources, and at least twelve months
before exhausting all her foreign exchange resources."
Although Japan is not likely to pursue a war of aggres-
sion up to such point of exhaustion, it still possesses
exohange

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.98)
4
exohange resources which, barring foreign intervention,
are probably sufficient to enable her to obtain the
terms or controls she desires in China.
Gold Resources. Although Japan has exported approxi-
mately $245 million in gold during 1937, the Bank of Japan
has so far maintained intact its gold reserve of $261
million as established at the time of yen devaluation
(August 25). In addition, the gold holdings of the
Government's exchange stabilisation fund are estimated
to be in the neighborhood of $40 million at the end of
December. Gold shipments from this fund to maintain
the present exohange value of the yen are being replen-
1shed in part by Government purchases of newly mined
gold in the Japanese Empire, whose production averaged
about $4 million monthly during 1937 and is expected to
be increased in 1938 under the operation of the recent
gold production law. Strenuous efforts are being made
to expand gold production in Manchukuo (estimated at
$4 1/2 million in 1937) whioh could be used to finance
in part imports for the Kwantung Army.
Bank Balances Abroad. Although Japanese importers
are experiencing increasing difficulty in obtaining bank
or commercial credits abroad, a recent check made by the
Treasury Department indicated that Japanese bank balances
in

=== Page 415 === (confidence 1.00)
5
in New York amounted to $45.2 million as of December 8.
These appear to have been built up largely by recent
gold shipments to this country, and in view of the fact
that during the three months September through November
United States imports from Japan came within $6 1/2
million of balancing exports to that country, a fairly
substantial margin of ready dollar exchange appears to
be available for financing essential excess imports
during the next couple of months.
Foreign Security Holdings. According to the Japan-
ese Ministry of Finance, holdings of foreign government
or corporate securities by Japanese nationals at home
and abroad, as of April 1937, amounted to 389 million
yen (at old par of exchange) or roughly $200 million.
It is not known how iarge a proportion of Chinese
obligations are included in this total, nor what amount
of other foreign securities may have been sold since
that date, but it is probable that something like $75
million in foreign exchange might still be realised
through the liquidation of such assets, and possibly
another $25 million through the gradual selling of out-
standing Japanese foreign currency obligations owned by
Japanese nationals and institutions.
Japan's total foreign exchange resources, in the
form

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
6
form of gold, foreign bank balances and foreign securi-
ties, are estimated to be equivalent to about $475 mil-
lion, which, 1f utilised at the rate of 40 to 50 million
dollars a month, might potentially carry her through the
greater part of 1938, although this would entail out-
right exhaustion of her extremely liquid resources and
virtually complete dissipation of her gold currency
reserves.
Japan's Balance of Payments. Japan was well pre-
pared for the outbreak of hostilities in midsummer
through the building up of large stocks of war and
industrial raw materials by heavy excess imports in the
first half of the year. By the end of August her trade
deficit amounted to ¥ 775 million, compared to ¥ 265
million in the first eight months of 1936. Since Aug-
ust, by drawing on her reserve stocks, by rigidly con-
trolling imports, especially of non-essentials, by
curtailing domestic consumption, and vigorously pushing
export sales, Japan has been able to show an excess of
exports during September-November, thereby reducing her
unfavorable trade balance for the eleven months of 1937
to  650 million. Lower prices for imported raw
materials have also been a factor in Japan's recently
improved trade position. The prospect for ensuing
months

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.99)
7
months is less favorable, owing to the necessity of
replenishing depleted stocks and the difficulties of
maintaining exports in the face of restrictions on
imports of materials entering into manufactures, diver-
sion of labor and capital into war purposes, rising
domestic costs, and anti-Japanese boycott movements
abroad. Moreover, Japan's normally favorable invisible
balance of payments (reported for 1936 as * 233 million,
exclusive of capital movements) is being substantially
curtailed through the diversion of Japanese shipping to
war transport service, disappearance of returns from
direct investments in China and liquidation of holdings
of foreign securities. Despite these adverse develop-
ments and prospects in Japan's international balance of
payments, her exchange resources and control measures
will enable her to carry on for some months unless
economic sanctions are applied against her or foreign
military intervention develops.
Concluding Observations.
Although the early conclusion of a peace with China
would undoubtedly ease the strain on Japan's financial
resources, this pressure does not yet appear to be suf-
ficiently critical, in the absence of foreign economic
or military intervention, to compel an immiment deflec-
tion

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.97)
8
tion of Japanese objectives or efforts in China. The
more serious difficulties confronting Japan are likely
to arise after the termination of active hostilities.
Mr. Anderson is warranted in pointing out towards the
end of his article that the replacement of Japan's
destroyed industrial enterprises in China, the stupen-
dous task of reconstruction in ravaged areas, and the
development of natural resources brought under Japanese
control, will involve demands for capital which cannot
be met from Japan's own depleted and debt-burdened
resources. It is this dependence on foreign financial
participation which appears to offer the prospect of
settlement on terms which may minimise Chinese sabotage
and leave the door sufficiently open to attract foreign
capital. Such consummation is likely to be delayed,
however, so long as military considerations remein
paramount in Japan and it is not dictated by Japan's
immediate financial position.
EA:HSP:EB
H

Washington, D.C.,
Dear Secretary Hull:
DIVISION OF
COMMUNICATIONS
A column just read Aincthe New York Times impels me to write to
you. I am an American missionary, on furlough from China, whence I returned
last February. At that time, all seemed at peace.
Day before yesterday I received a letter written by a doctor of
our mission. He is an honor graduate from Yale, a missionary of many years'
experience in China as physician and surgeon, evangelist, and teacher, as wel
as statesman. Enclosed is a copy of this letter, which I am sharing with you
without his consent, and am therefore withholding his name, although you may hav
it if needed. When you read this letter, you will realize better than ever what
conditions obtain there, and are likely to be in the rest of the world, if
things go on as they are now. You will know also why I make the request that
follows innthis letter:
5
The Times says that Japan is asking for a credit of $50,000,00
with which to develop Manchukuoh with machinery bought on this credit. American
financiers should by all means refuse to grant this credit; because,
1. Money already in Japan will be released by this loan to be invested in materi
als used to prosecute the war with China. By all means we want as a nation
and as individuals to avoid helping continue this war.
2. Japan's word is not to be trusted. The $50,0o0,0o0 may in some way itself
be used in whole or in part for war materials.
3. Manchukuoh does not rightfully belong to Japan; the province would rather go
undeveloped than have it done by Japan; and it will be rightfully developed
and will co-operate willingly in its development when it is done by China and
its own people. Unless so done, it will delay such development as much as
/MR
possible.
C2
4. Japan does not want this money at this time, I dare say, to "develop Man-
chukuoh as we would wish to see it done; Japan is too busy with er war to
carry on the works of peace at present.
5. America cannot afford, from any angle, to help Japan against China in an
unrighteous war; whether we consider justice, or American investments in
China, or future prospects of peace, or simply the question of what is right
always the main issue,
That loan should be refused!
If it be said that "To refuse will be considered an unfriendly act," I
should answer:
1. America, because of her situation geographically and otherwise, need fear
outbreak of war against her less than any other country one can name.

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.98)
country must do something to change the present situation, or an even
eater wrong to China, and the world, an even more serious international
ituation, is before us.
If nothing is done to stop this war, America is bound to be drawn into war
in the end; for, sooner or later, war will spread.
+. Not every peopla and nation must fear Japan; fear of injustice and sin and
evil must be greater. People are expecting greater things of America in
this Eastern situation than we have seen thus far, and we must not continue
to disappoint them!
I ask you please to use every means in your power to keep our busi-
ness men from selling out our ideals and our hopes for $50,000,000.
Respectfully yours,
May Bel hompson.

Friends and Sharers in the work oft the Kingdom over here,
From present appearances it may not be
long before we shall not be able to write freely, and for that reason a gen
letter is soon agein going out to you to add a few matters of the situatio
in China, and of how it looks to us. Many people here are perfectly convino
that the conquest of China is only a kind of first step tpward the realizat
of the ambitions of Japan. The man whom I believe to be the best informed i
our city, a Britisher, and a friend of many Chinese officials said without
the slightest hesitation that the next move after taking China would be th
Philippines, Borneo, and move down toward Australia.
That the Japanese have been working up an anti-American sentiment in their
own land, 1s only too certain, and also that they in their language have
changed the mame for"America" They usedto call it by a name which means
"the beautiful country". Now they call it by a name which means "the rice
country", because they mean to eat it up like rice.
Please do not think that I coneern myself to any great extent with politio
matters. I have far too much to do in the medical and evangelistic work to
indulge in that, but it does seem to me that America ought to know what is
the prevailing sentiment out here.
By the time this letter reaches you, things will have changed vastly from
what they are to-day. At present the Japanese say that they want nothing be
yond the capital in Nanking, which means that this part of the world west 
it would not be invaded. At all events it would seem wise if they did stop
at Nanking for e while, that is, for their own good. The most recent news
which comes to us is that of the bombing of geveral cities between Shanghai
and Nank ing. Newspaper reports and the word of the people who have been 1n
few of these places bear evidence of the appalling loss of life among the o
lian population at Soochow. A foreign newspaper says that the planes which
dropped 7oo bombs on that beautiful and very populous city, went along overn
as one would sow a field of wheat, repeatine their flights in parallel col-
umns, and then they made parallelx lines at richt angles to these. A plao
called Chanchow on the railroad neurer to Nanking than oochow is, was also
bombed very terribly. A servant who was talking about it to one of the mis
sionaries staying at present in Fiukiang, was pitifully undone nervously by
the experience. This servant said that the place was bombed by 1oo Japanese
planes. Another city which is something of an industrial center, called Wus
is reported as very badly damaged or de troyed. I can't help wondering wheth
the civilized world is going to se ttle down peacefully to accept this sort o
thing with regard to civilian populations. That night when our city was bo
ed, we had warning quite a while before planes came. I was bracing my nerve
for the possible bombing which might be coming, but when it began, it was
something of a psychic shock, not alone, by any means, because of the crashe
involved, but to actually realize that people would rain missles of death on
a practically defenseless people and one that was so innocent into the barga
Please notice that I am not telling you the most incradibly awful stories wit
regard to the areas that have already been occupied by the Japanese. Some o
these things are perfectly fiendish, and I think are no doubt, in part at
least, propaganda. But I do want you to realize the dreadfulness of whole-
sale attacks upon cities. It is nothing but a return to barbarism of centu
bies ago. And it is a very vital question as to just what the civilized
world is going to do about it. Only a few days ago a letter came from a
friend in New York State referring to conditions here in China, and obviously
